
# MADHYA PRADESH DISTRICT GAZETTEERS: VIDISHA

**By P. N. SHRIVASTAV & Dr. RAJENDRA VERMA** *DISTRICT GAZETTEERS DEPARTMENT, MADHYA PRADESH, BHOPAL* *First Edition: 1979*

---

## PREFACE

The present volume is the fifteenth in the series of the Madhya Pradesh District Gazetteers, being written under a scheme jointly sponsored by the Government of India and the Government of Madhya Pradesh.

The task of preparing the Gazetteer of Vidisha District was beset with difficulties. The District, as constituted today, comprises not only the Bhilsa District of the former Gwalior State and the small princely States of Kurwai, Muhammadgarh and Pathari, but also the Sironj Sub-division of the Kota District of Rajasthan and a small tract of the former Bhopal State. Being separate entities, they had different history and were governed through different set of rules. The Gwalior State Gazetteer, published in 1907, containing material on the entire Gwalior State, including parts of modern Vidisha District, was of little help in this work. It was rather difficult to get facts and figures concerning the small princely States. The discernible reader will, we feel sure, realise, that the creation of an integrated picture regarding the various topics was, in these circumstances, a formidable task. The work was, however, rendered possible owing to the valuable help we received from the various Departments of Government as well as from the different institutions and individuals.

Vidisha has a hoary past, with a rich archaeology and history. The archaeological wealth scattered all over the District, speaks of the glory that was Vidisha. It has been referred to in the Epics, the Puranas and in the Buddhist, Jain and Brahmanical literature. The religious tolerance of the age is reflected here in the numerous Brahmanical, Buddhist and Jain remains, which coexisted. The Heliodoros Pillar or the Garuddhwaja at Besnagar, that was ancient Vidisha of the Sunga period, the Udayagiri caves of the Gupta period, the magnificient temples at Gyaraspur, Udayapur and Badoh symbolise the rich cultural heritage of this District. It was a rich and prosperous trading centre in ancient period. The economic prosperity of this area was retained atleast till the days of the Imperial Guptas, for in his *Meghaduta*, Kalidasa has referred to Vidisha as a place where everybody gets wealth to his heart's content. This economic prosperity was due to its advantageous position on the cross-roads of two important trade routes. One of these connected Mathura with Pratisthan (modern Paithan on the Godavari) via-Vidisha and another route, passing through this place, connected Pataliputra in the east with Bharukachchha (modern Broach), in the west.

This District is also closely associated with Emperor Asoka. It was at Vidisha that Asoka married Devi, a banker's daughter. Her son, Mahendra, and daughter Sanghamitra, are famous in history as their father's religious ambassadors to Sri Lanka. They are known to have carried a twig of the original Bodhi tree and the Buddha's message of love, compassion and universal brotherhood to that country.

The draft of Vidisha District Gazetteer was finalised by the former State Editor, Shri P. N. Shrivastav in the year 1971-72. But before the chapters could be cyclostyled for being sent to the Government of India and the members of the State Advisory Board, Dr. Rajendra Verma was appointed State Editor. During the latter's tenure certain chapters were pruned. It is, therefore, in the fitness of things to publish this volume in the name of both the former State Editors. The draft was approved by the Government of India in February, 1973 and it was sent to press in the month of June, 1976.

## CHAPTER I: GENERAL

### Location and Extent
Vidisha District extends between the latitude 23° 21' and 24° 22′ north and longitude 77° 15′30 and 78° 18′ east. The District is situated in the central part of the State and eastern part of the fertile Malwa region. The shape of this District is more or less elliptical and the longer axis lies from N.W. to S.E. with slight projections on the north, north-west, south and south-west. Its greatest length from north-west to south-east is about 133.6 km. and the greatest width from north-east to south-west is about 96 km. The Tropic of Cancer passes through the southern stretch of the District, about 2 km. south of the District headquarters. It is bounded in the north and north-east by Guna district, in the south by Raisen district, in the east by Sagar district and in the south-west by Sehore district.

### Area and Population
The area of the District is 7,433 sq. km. The population of the District is 6,58,427 (with provisional figures in 1971 Census being 6,56,011).

### Origin of the Name
The District derives its name from the headquarters town of Vidisha. The earliest reference of Vidisha is contained in the Ramayana by Valmiki. It is stated there that Shatrughana's son Shatrughati was placed in charge of Vidisha. The place has also been mentioned in the Mahabharat. The historicity of the ancient city of Besnagar, three kilometres from modern Vidisha and identified with ancient Vidisha, goes back to some centuries before the birth of Christ. Besnagar figures prominently in Buddhist, Jain and Brahmanical literature in various forms such as Vessanagar, Vaisyanagar, etc. Tradition connects the town with Raja Rukmangada who, neglecting his own wife for the Apsara Visva, named the town Vishvanagar after her. The Skanda Puran refers to Vidisha as a tirth (holy place) which should be visited after visiting Someshwar. The Brihata Samhita also makes a mention of Vidisha.

On the destruction of Besnagar, located on the western side of the Betwa sometime after 7th century A.D., a new town sprang up on the eastern bank of the river. This new town was known as Bhailaswamin or Bhillaswamin, so named after the sun god, to whom a temple was dedicated here. The name of the place was later corrupted to Bhilsa, when Al Beruni visited the town in 11th century A.D. The Jain scriptures use the form Bhadalpur, and regard it as the holy place (tirth) of Sital Nath, the tenth Tirthankar of Jain religion.

The Ain-i-Akbari mentions Bhilsa (Vidisha) and Sironj, as Mahals under the Sarkars (Districts) of Raisen and Chanderi, respectively. Ganj Basoda (a tahsil headquarters) was also a district during the 19th century. In Gwalior State, Vidisha (Bhilsa) was a tahsil of Isagarh pargana. In 1904 Vidisha was raised to a District having two tahsils of Vidisha and Basoda till the formation of Madhya Bharat in 1948. The District was enlarged in 1949 by the merger of small States of Kurwai, Mohammadgarh and Pathari which formed Kurwai Tahsil.

The Sironj sub-division with two tahsils of Sironj and Lateri, formerly in Kota district of Rajasthan State, and the small Pargana of Piklon consisting of 11 villages belonging to Bhopal State were added to the District in 1956 with the formation of new Madhya Pradesh. At the same time the town and the District were renamed as Vidisha.

The former State of Kurwai was divided into four parganas in 1900. In 1918-19 there were two tahsils in the State, viz., Kurwai and Sherwasa. Of these, one formed a compact unit while the other was much scattered and interlaced with Gwalior territory. In 1952, twelve villages of Kurwai tahsil (Area 21.2 sq. miles) were transferred to Mungaoli tahsil of Guna district, while 10 villages of Bhopal State were included in the Kurwai Tahsil.

#### Sironj Sub-Division
Sironj was a kingdom of Raja Roopan in Alha's times. 'Sironj' has been mentioned thrice in the famous Alha Khand. Sengar Rajput Shankarsingh is said to have developed this place, which was known as Sengaraj, corrupted to Sironj. The Vakiyat of Tonk also supports this view. Since the conquest of Malwa by Sher Shah Sur, Sironj was kept as a pargana and ruled through an Amil (Administrator) under the Sarkar of Chanderi. The Mughals maintained this arrangement. The Barodah village in Sironj tahsil was also a Mahal in Sarkar Chanderi during Akbar's reign.

Sironj had come into the possession of the Peshwas through Chhatrasal and it was with the Holkars from A.D. 1757. Yashwantrao Holkar then gave Sironj to Amin-ud-Dowlah in A.D. 1788, and it remained under his descendents till 1948. The State of Tonk included the Pargana of Sironj. The State entered into Subsidiary Alliance with the British on the 9th November, 1817. With this alliance the Aligarh Pargana (Rampur) was awarded to the Nawab.

In the year 1892 there were four tahsils in the Pargana, viz., Lateri, Anandpur, Devapur and Deepanakhedal. From 1898 the three tahsils of the Pargana were Sadar, Eastern and Western. The Amil or the Administrator of the Pargana had almost all powers for local administration. These powers were reduced after the rebellion of the Amil in 1835. It was during the rule of Nawab Ibrahim Ali Khan that the designation of Amil was changed to Nazim, who enjoyed magisterial powers, but the Police and Judiciary were made separate branches of administration.

On the 30th April, 1948, Sironj alongwith Tonk State was merged in the United Rajasthan State. The pargana was made a district with two tahsils at Sironj and Lateri. The third tahsil, Siyalpur was merged in Sironj. However, on the 7th April, 1949, the present State of Rajasthan was formed and in 1950, the District was abolished and made a Sub-Division of Kota district. On the 1st November, 1956, Sironj Sub-Division was transferred to new Madhya Pradesh and was merged in Vidisha District.

### Sub-Divisions and Tahsils
Vidisha (Bhilsa) was a part of Prant Malwa of the former Gwalior State. It was a part of Ujjain Division and then of Gwalior Division in former Madhya Bharat. Since the formation of Madhya Pradesh, Vidisha remains a part of Bhopal Division. At present Vidisha District is divided into five tahsils, viz., Vidisha, Basoda, Kurwai, Sironj and Lateri. There is a residential Sub-Divisional Officer at Sironj with Sironj, Kurwai and Lateri Tahsils in his charge. The Sub-Divisional Officers for Vidisha and Basoda have their headquarters at Vidisha.

### Topography
The District of Vidisha lies on the Vindhyachal plateau, off the main Vindhyachal range, which throws several spurs towards the north and north-east. The plateau slopes towards the north and is drained by a number of rivers of Malwa and Bundelkhand. These rivers have formed their valleys between the spur ranges. Most of Vidisha lies in the valley of the Betwa river, which flows from south to north. This valley is bordered by the Garhi-Teonda range in the east and Ganiari-Raghogarh range in the west. Both these ranges form part of the ranges of Vindhyachal on the Malwa plateau and extend from south to north.

The physical features of the District can be divided into five divisions:
1.  **The Valley of the Betwa proper:** Extends from south to north in the central part of the District and is 50 to 65 km. wide. It includes the Betwa valley and the valleys of its tributaries like the Besh, Bah, Sagar, and Kethan in the west, and Nion and Keotan in the east. The soil is rich black silt derived from basalt rocks, making it a major wheat-growing tract.
2.  **The Bina Valley:** A narrow belt along the eastern boundary separated from the Betwa valley by the Garhi-Teonda range. It includes the valleys of the Babuai and Bagaru streams.
3.  **The Eastern Range (Garhi-Teonda range):** Extends south to north between the Betwa and Bina valleys. The highest peak is Lakholi (665.7 meters). The average elevation is 520 meters. It is rich in high-quality sedimentary rocks, sandstone, and limestone.
4.  **The Western Range:** A denuded spur crossing Lateri tahsil and forming a tableland in western Sironj. Notable peaks include Chopra (557.8 m), Lateri (542.5 m), and Suran Tal (548.2 m). Covered mostly by poor quality mixed forests.
5.  **The Sindh Valley:** Enclosed by the Western range and an off-shoot from Nainwas to Surantal, separating its waters from the Keotan and Parbati. Anandpur is located in this valley.

### Drainage
The entire District lies in the drainage basin of the Yamuna. The general slope is from south to north.
* **The Betwa (Betrawati):** Rises in the extreme south-west of Raisen district. It enters Vidisha 6 km south of the town and flows for about 112 km within the district. Significant western tributaries include the Besh, Bah, Sagar, and Kethan; eastern tributaries include Nion, Parasri, and Bina.
* **The Bes (Besh / Halali):** Rises near Parawalia in Sehore district, flows north-east, serves as a boundary with Raisen district, and joins the Betwa 3 km north of Vidisha. Its total length is 65 km (15 km in the district).
* **The Bah:** Rises from Hinotia hill in Sehore district and serves as a boundary between Basoda and Vidisha tahsils. It provides water through dry months for many villages.
* **The Sagar:** Rises 6 km south-east of Lateri and flows 51 km east to join the Betwa near Jodh.
* **The Naren:** A small river rising near Sironj and flowing 40 km to meet the Betwa near Sarera.
* **The Kethan:** Rises from Sakhlon hill and flows 35 km north-east before entering Guna district and joining the Betwa at the border.
* **The Bina:** Rises in Raisen district, flows north separating Sagar from Vidisha, and receives the Babnai and Bagaru on its left bank.
* **The Nihar (Nion):** Rises from the Garhi range and joins the Betwa near Sumer.
* **The Keotan:** Rises from Mahuakhera hill and joins the Betwa on its right bank.
* **The Sind:** Rises about 8 km south-east of Lateri. Out of its 400 km course, only the first 35 km lie in this district.

### Geology & Minerals
The main geological formations occurring in Vidisha are **Alluvium**, **Laterite**, and **Deccan Trap (Malwa Trap)** with inter-trappean beds.
* **Limestone:** Inter-trappean limestone deposits exist 58 km from Ganj Basoda station, with reserves estimated at 50,000 tons.
* **Vindhyan Sandstones:** Extensively quarried in Kaimur and Rewa formations, offering excellent building material used for massive structures and delicate carvings.
* **Basalts:** Deccan trap basalts are used for ordinary building stones in village huts and foundations.
* **Green Earth:** Found in the cavities of the trap rock on the bank of the Betwa near Bijrota (Bijrota is 1.6 km north of Bareth station).
* **Ground Water:** Water levels in the trap country vary from 6 to 12 meters, while in the Vindhyan formations, conditions are less satisfactory with water tables deeper than 15 meters, often drying up in summer.

### Earthquakes
Vidisha is located in **Zone 1** of the Earthquake Zoning Map of India. It has experienced fringe effects of major earthquakes from Kutch (1819), Assam (1897), Kangra (1905), and the Bihar-Nepal Border (1934), all measuring around magnitude 8 to 8.5, but causing no significant structural damage locally.

### Flora and Fauna
The forests cover about two-fifths of the total district area (89,811 Hectares across Gyaraspur, Shamshabad, Lateri, and Sironj ranges). The forest type is **Southern Tropical Dry Deciduous**, with predominantly Teak crops on Deccan trap formations, and poorer mixed forests on Vindhyan formations. Khair (*Acacia catechu*) dominates the lateritic soils in the Sironj range. Bamboos (*Dendrocalamus strictus*) are localized near the Dudhia river.

Common mammals include the Indian hare, black buck (rare due to poaching), chinkara, barking deer, sambhar, chital, wild boar, leopard, tiger, nilgai, and monkey. Wildlife census data from 1968 reported small populations of tigers and panthers across the Gyaraspur, Shamshabad, and Sironj ranges. Birds include the pea-fowl (national bird), red jungle-fowl, grey pigeon, spotted dove, and the *mahoka* (southern crow-pheasant), which is locally associated with honey (*madhu*).

### Climate
The climate is generally dry except during the south-west monsoon (mid-June to September). Summers are less intense than in North India, with a mean daily maximum around 40°C in May. Winters are moderate, though cold waves can occasionally bring temperatures close to freezing. The average annual rainfall is **1,229.9 mm**, increasing from north-west to south-east. The heaviest recorded 24-hour rainfall was **360.2 mm** at Basoda on 31st July, 1951.

---

## CHAPTER II: HISTORY

### Prehistory
Palaeolithic tools (choppers, handaxes, cleavers) discovered in the valleys of the Betwa, Bina, Dhasan, and Keotan indicate ancient prehistoric settlements. Chalcolithic tools dating back to 1000–700 B.C. were found in ancient mounds alongside painted black-and-red pottery, signifying subsequent Aryan influence.

### Classical & Mythological Era
Vidisha is mentioned as a *janapada* in the Puranas and as a holy *tirtha* in the Skanda Purana. The Ramayana states that Shatrughana's son Shatrughati was placed in charge of Vidisha, while Kalidasa's *Raghuvamsam* notes that Shatrughna expelled the Yadavas and placed his son Subahu here. The ancient capital of the region (known as Dasharna) was **Besnagar**, located three kilometers from modern Vidisha on the western bank of the Betwa river. Vidisha grew into a wealthy trading hub situated at the crossroads of two major ancient trade routes: one connecting Mathura to Pratisthan (Paithan) and another connecting Pataliputra to Bharukachchha (Broach).

### The Mauryas & Sungas
Asoka, while serving as Viceroy of Ujjain, married **Devi**, a banker's daughter from Vidisha. Her children Mahendra and Sanghamitra led the famous Buddhist mission to Sri Lanka. After the decline of the Mauryas, Vidisha temporarily emerged as an independent City State, striking its own copper coins reading *Vedisa*. Pushyamitra Sunga established control in 187 B.C., and his son **Agnimitra** ruled from Vidisha as Viceroy. Agnimitra's exploits and his love for Malavika are immortalized in Kalidasa's play *Malavikagnimitram*.

During the reign of the Sunga king Bhagabhadra, the Greek king Antialcidas of Taxila sent his ambassador **Heliodorus**, who erected the famous **Garuda Pillar (Khambaba)** at Besnagar in honor of Lord Vasudeva, providing early archaeological proof of Vaishnava culture in the 2nd century B.C.

### Later Dynasties
* **Satavahanas & Kshatrapas:** Control shifted between the southern Satavahanas (Gautamiputra Satakarni) and the Western Kshatrapas (Chastana and Rudradaman) during the 1st and 2nd centuries A.D.
* **The Nagas & Vakatakas:** The Nagas established themselves by the 3rd century A.D. Concurrently, the Puranas mention Vindhyasakti, the founder of the Vakataka dynasty, as a ruler of Vidisha.
* **The Guptas:** Samudragupta incorporated the region into the Gupta Empire. Chandragupta II (Vikramaditya) left undisputed evidence of his sovereignty through the rock inscriptions at the **Udayagiri hills** dated 401 A.D., recorded by his minister Virasena and a feudatory Maharaja Sanakanika. Kalidasa adorned his court and praised Vidisha in the *Meghaduta*.
* **Kalachuris & Paramaras:** The early Kalachuris controlled the region in the 6th century A.D. (proven by Buddharaja's Vadner grant issued from Vidisha in 608 A.D.). After the 7th century, Besnagar was devastated by a massive flood, and the population shifted across the river to establish **Bhilsa** (named after the *Bhaillasvamin* Sun temple). The Paramara king Bhoja Deva (1000–1055 A.D.) ruled over Bhilsa, and his successor Udayaditya built the magnificent **Nilkanthesvar temple at Udaypur**.

### Muslim Conquest & Malwa Sultans
In 1234 A.D., Sultan Iltutmish sacked Bhilsa and destroyed the great Vijay Mandir temple. In 1293 A.D., Ala-ud-din Khalji captured the town and sent rich spoils to Delhi. Malwa later became an independent kingdom under Dilawar Khan Ghuri (1401 A.D.) and the Khalji dynasty. During the decline of the Khaljis, a local Purabiya chief named **Salhadi** held Bhilsa and Raisen. In 1532 A.D., Bahadur Shah of Gujarat besieged Raisen and sacked Bhilsa, putting it under Muslim governors.

### The Mughals & Marathas
Akbar annexed Malwa into the Mughal Empire in 1562 A.D. Bhilsa became a mahal under the Sarkar of Raisen, and Sironj under the Sarkar of Chanderi. Aurangzeb destroyed the rebuilt Vijay Mandir in 1682, erected a mosque on its site, and officially renamed the town *Alamgirpur* (though the name never caught on).

During the decline of the Mughals, Dost Muhammad Khan of Bhopal seized Vidisha and Gyaraspur by killing the governor Muhammad Faruk. By 1737 A.D., the Marathas under Peshwa Baji Rao expanded into Malwa. Tukoji and Jeevaji Pawar besieged and took Vidisha in January 1737. Sironj became a key Maratha base where Naro Shankar and Pilaji Jadhav resided to collect revenues. In 1761, while staying at Vidisha, Peshwa Balaji Baji Rao intercepted the letter carrying the tragic news of the Maratha defeat at the Battle of Panipat.

### First Anglo-Maratha War & Amir Khan Pindari
During the First Anglo-Maratha War, Mahadaji Sindhia took direct command and soundly defeated the retreating British forces just south of Sironj in March 1781. In 1798, Sironj was assigned by Jaswant Rao Holkar to his ally **Amir Khan Pindari**. Following British campaigns to suppress the Pindaris, the Treaty of 1817 was signed, guaranteeing Amir Khan the state of Tonk, of which Sironj remained a detached sub-division.

### The Great Revolt of 1857
The district witnessed severe conflict during 1857. **Fazil Muhammad Khan** and **Adil Muhammad Khan**, the Jagirdars of Ambapani, raised the banner of revolt. They sacked Pathari and arrested its Nawab. In early 1858, Sir Hugh Rose marched to suppress the rebellion; Fazil Muhammad Khan was captured at Rahatgarh and hanged. Adil Muhammad Khan eluded capture, joined Tatya Tope, and waged guerrilla warfare across the Vidisha and Sironj jungles (occupying forts at Bussul and Pipria). Despite British expeditions led by Lt. Bruce and Capt. Roome, which burned rebel strongholds like Gunapura and Ghatira, Adil Muhammad managed to escape towards Lalitpur, while the remnants of his force surrendered by September 1859.

### Freedom Movement & Integration
National consciousness grew under organizations like the Sarvajanik Sabha (1929), with Takhtmal Jain from Vidisha serving as its Secretary. Picketings and Satyagraha were organized during the Quit India Movement of 1942 by leaders like Ram Sahay, Ramchandra Naval, and Madan Devi Naval.

Following India's Independence, the Madhya Bharat Union was formed in May 1948, integrating the Gwalior and Kurwai states, with Takhtmal Jain eventually serving as Chief Minister. Sironj and Lateri remained part of Rajasthan until the **Reorganisation of States on 1st November, 1956**, when they were transferred to Madhya Pradesh and officially integrated into the newly renamed Vidisha District.

---

## CHAPTER III: PEOPLE

### Population Structure
According to the 1971 Census, the population stands at **6,58,427** (349,767 males and 308,660 females) spread across 1,510 villages and 4 towns (Vidisha, Sironj, Basoda, and Kurwai). The population density is 89 persons per sq. km (lower than the state average of 94). The population is predominantly rural (85.84%), with the urban component at 14.16%.

The sex-ratio stands at **882 females per 1,000 males**. Age-group data from 1961 shows a sharp decline in the female ratio from 989 (ages 0-4) down to 628 (ages 55-59), indicating high female mortality in later years, though a reverse trend appears past the age of 70, where females outnumber males (1,100 per 1,000).

### Language & Bilingualism
**Hindi** is the primary language, spoken by 90.95% of the population (1961 data). **Urdu** is the second most prominent language (7.84%), with its highest concentrations in the rural areas of Kurwai (12%), Sironj (11%), and Lateri (9%). **Marathi** (0.36%), Marwari, Sindhi, and Punjabi are also spoken. Historically, *Bundeli* was the dominant dialect in 1908, shifting to *Malwi* in the 1921–1941 censuses, before standard Hindi became widely returned.

### Religion & Caste
The religious breakup (1971) shows **Hindus** at 89.21%, **Muslims** at 9.03%, and **Jains** at 1.65%, followed by small numbers of Sikhs, Christians, and Buddhists.

The Scheduled Castes form about 23% and Scheduled Tribes form 5.4% of the population (1971).
* **Scheduled Castes:** The most numerous are the **Chamars** (including synonymous groups like Jatav, Ahirwar, Mochi), followed by Koris, Chidars, Bhangis (Mehtars), and Basods. The Basods are traditionally engaged in manufacturing bamboo articles.
* **Scheduled Tribes:** The **Saharias** (Seharia/Sahariya) form the principal tribe (86% of tribals). Historically, Saharias suffered under oppressive agricultural labor systems like *Hari Batholi* (lump-sum debt bondage) and *Hali Banta* (wages exclusively in kind), effectively rendering them bound laborers. Gonds, Korkus, and Minas (localized in Sironj tahsil, originating from Rajasthan) also live in the district.

### New Religious Movements: Taran Swami
Vidisha is closely associated with **Taran Swami** (born 1448 A.D.), a Jain saint who founded the Taranpanthi sect. He renounced the world and settled in Semarkhedi. Emphasizing self-knowledge and soul-worship (*"the real worship of God is the worship of the soul"*), he fiercely preached against religious dogmatism, external rituals, and caste barriers, authoring 14 books in Apabhramsha (e.g., *Shravakachar*, *Pandit Puja*, *Malaji*). His primary pilgrimage center is at **Nisai Semalkhedi** (near Sironj), where followers gather during Basant Panchami.

### Social Life & Customs
* **Joint Families:** Nuclear and limited joint family patterns dominate. Large joint families have fragmented due to high living costs and changing occupations. Average family sizes range from 5.5 persons (rural) to 6.9 persons (urban) per house.
* **Marriage & Morals:** Monogamy is the standard, though polygamy is traditionally found among tribals due to high female participation in farm labor. Cross-cousin marriage (*dudhlautana*) is preferred among Gonds. Remarriage of widows is permitted among lower castes and tribals (known as *natra* or *baithna*). Divorce is traditionally handled via caste panchayats rather than civil courts; a financial compensation known as *jhagde-ki-rakam* is settled by the new husband to dissolve an old marriage.
* **Home Life & Food:** Village houses are primarily ground-floor mud structures (*kachcha*), while *pakka* stone/brick houses are common in towns. Wheat is the staple food, supplemented by milk. Poorer classes consume coarse grains like *bajra*, *jowar*, or gram bread, along with a porridge called *maheri* or *rabri*.
* **Folk Art & Amusements:** Professional folk dancers called *bednis* perform the **Rai dance**, a characteristic romantic, fast-paced torch-light dance of the region accompanied by instruments like the dholak, manjire, and algoza. Important annual fairs include the Lateri Mela (December), Sankrant fair at Rajghat Barat, and the Ramlila fairs at Basoda and Gyaraspur.

---

## CHAPTER IV: AGRICULTURE AND IRRIGATION

Agriculture is the economic mainstay; 83.42% of total workers were cultivators or agricultural labourers in 1971. Vidisha boasts the highest per capita net sown area in Madhya Pradesh (1.94 acres), characterized by a high proportion of large land holdings exceeding 10 to 50 acres.

### Land Utilization (1971-72)
* **Total Geographical Area (via village papers):** 730,000 Hectares
* **Net Area Sown:** 491,000 Hectares
* **Forests:** 81,000 Hectares
* **Total Cropped Area:** 501,000 Hectares
* **Culturable Waste:** 38,000 Hectares

### Reclamation of Land: The Kans Problem
Large tracts of fertile land were historically abandoned due to the rapid infestation of a destructive weed called **Kans**. To combat this, the Government launched a massive reclamation scheme during the First Five Year Plan utilizing heavy machinery from the **Central Tractor Organisation (CTO)** alongside state tractor units. Over 137,000 acres of infested land were successfully liberated, encouraging private farmers to purchase tractors and expand the frontiers of cultivation.

### Irrigation Facilities
Irrigation historically played a minor role due to the naturally high moisture-retention capacity of the black cotton soil (*mar*). However, intense cultivation has accelerated irrigation projects.
* **Canals & Tanks:** Canal systems have rapidly overtaken traditional well irrigation. Major projects include the **Kethan Medium Irrigation Project** (envisaging an 18-meter high dam to irrigate 5,000 acres) and the **Halali Major Irrigation Project** (designed to irrigate 81,600 acres across Vidisha and Raisen via 64 miles of canals).
* **Lift Irrigation:** Massive electric irrigation pumps have been installed along the major perennial rivers; Vidisha and Basoda operate over 115 heavy lift-pumps directly on the Betwa river.
* **Soil Conservation:** Deforestation has dropped sub-soil water levels. Extensive contour-bunding and dry farming methods have been adopted across hundreds of thousands of acres.

### Crop Patterns
Vidisha is fundamentally a **Rabi crop area** (Rabi to Kharif ratio is 65:35).
* **Wheat:** The primary crop, occupying 50% of the entire cropped area due to the nutrient-rich black alluvial soil.
* **Gram & Jowar:** Command 18% and 8% of the area, respectively. Jowar is the largest Kharif crop.
* **Poppy Discontinuance:** Opium poppy cultivation, which once formed a thriving commercial trade in Bhilsa during the 19th century, was completely phased out after Independence, releasing the prime irrigated lands for essential food grain production.
* **Mixed Sowing:** Common practices include co-planting Jowar with Tuar, Maize with Urad, and Wheat with Linseed. Traditional field preparation relies heavily on the *bakhar* (weeding harrow plough) before monsoon sowings (*boni*).

# VIDISHA DISTRICT GAZETTEER — PART 1B

## CHAPTER IV: AGRICULTURE AND IRRIGATION (CONTINUED)

### Agricultural Operations
All the operations are carried out with reference to the rainfall and in the manner in which they are generally performed in all other places of the Malwa tract [cite: 2203]. In Malwa, fields are first broken up and cleared of weeds by means of the *bakhar*, a sort of harrow or weeding plough [cite: 2204]. Ploughing (*halna*) for the autumn crops then commences about the end of May [cite: 2205]. As soon as sufficient rain has fallen to moisten the soil, the field is sown (*boni*) [cite: 2206]. The spring crops are sown till October, the ground being ploughed several times in order to enable the land to absorb as much moisture as possible [cite: 2207, 2211]. A local adage also testifies to it:

> *"Kartik mahat hal joto, tang pasar ghar manhi soto"* This means that by ploughing even in night hours (early morning) in the month of Kartik, one is not required to bother for the rest of the period and can enjoy undisturbed sleep [cite: 2212]. Small seeds such as sesamum, linseed, etc., are sown broadcast, but the larger classes of seed, such as jowar and maize, are sown through a *nai* or seed-drill attached to a plough [cite: 2212].

The next process is weeding (*nindai* or *dorni*) [cite: 2212]. This is done when the crop is quite small by means of a *khurpa*, and later on when the plant is about two feet high, with a *dora* or small plough [cite: 2212, 2213]. The *dora* is passed down between the rows of standing plants so as to remove foreign growths and loosen the soil at the roots [cite: 2213]. The plants are then thinned out (*galni*) if necessary [cite: 2213]. The final field process is reaping (*katni*) [cite: 2213].

Different crops are gathered in different ways [cite: 2215]. Jowar is cut by men standing, only the upper part of the plant being taken [cite: 2214, 2215]. Wheat and most other crops are cut close to the ground by persons stooping [cite: 2215, 2216]. Gram is pulled up by the roots [cite: 2216]. The crops are then taken to the threshing floor (*khala*) where they are either trodden out under the feet of oxen or threshed with a flail (*mogri*) [cite: 2217]. The former process, called *dawan*, is employed in the case of most crops, the latter process being applied only in the case of tuar and a few other grains [cite: 2218]. The grain is then winnowed and collected [cite: 2219].

#### Wheat (Triticum sativum)
Wheat commands the agricultural scene of the District [cite: 2220]. Wheat was sown in 5,30,204 acres during the year 1961-62, while its area was only 2,04,003 acres in 1951-52 [cite: 2220]. This increase within a decade, even after taking into account the integration of Sironj, is considerable [cite: 2221]. It marks a reversal of the declining trend which had earlier set in during the 'forties' and 'fifties' [cite: 2221]. Wheat and gram were the dominant crops of Bhilsa District [cite: 2222]. Inspite of the dependence on one crop, namely wheat, the District showed a diminution of this crop from the average of 5,54,339 acres for the five years from 1934-35 to 1938-39 to only 2,08,269 acres in 1943-44 [cite: 2223]. Later figures show a steady increase in the area, which had risen to 5,50,712 acres in 1964-65 [cite: 2224]. The area devoted to cultivation of wheat was 1,94,656 hectares in 1967-68 and increased to 235.5 thousand ha in 1972-73 [cite: 2225].

In some parts of the District, it is raised in submerged tanks [cite: 2226]. The outturn of wheat has similarly shown an enormous increase from 20.5 thousand tons in 1951-52 to 65.5 thousand tons in 1956-57 and 159.7 thousand tons in 1961-62 [cite: 2227]. The year 1967-68 gave an outturn of 155.7 thousand metric tons and 1972-73 an outturn of 166.4 thousand metric tons [cite: 2228]. 1966-67 was not a good year, and outturns were low, including that of wheat [cite: 2229]. In 1971-72, the current yield of wheat was 889 kg per hectare as against the standard of 560 kg [cite: 2230].

#### Gram (Cicer arietinum)
Next in importance to wheat is gram with 1,76,519 acres in 1956-57 and almost the same level in 1964 [cite: 2233]. In 1967-68, the area occupied by gram was 83,552 hectares, short by 16,805 ha in comparison to the previous year [cite: 2233, 2234]. Thus gram was responsible for 17 per cent of the cropped area in 1967-68 [cite: 2234]. Gram, however, occupied 94.9 thousand ha in 1972-73 [cite: 2235]. Gram is also the most important pulse crop, accounting for 75 per cent of the total area under pulses in 1971-72 [cite: 2236, 2237]. The other pulse crops (in 1971-72) are tuar (4.7 thousand ha), moong-moth (6.9 thousand ha), teora (8.0 thousand ha), and lentil (9.3 thousand ha) [cite: 2238].

Gram is produced in a quantity far in excess of local consumption [cite: 2239]. The large quantities exported outside the State make it almost a substitute for a cash crop in the District [cite: 2240]. Of the varieties common in cultivation, *Gulabi Chana* has the largest demand for exports and hence is the most lucrative and favored one [cite: 2241]. Gram gave an outturn of 43.3 thousand metric tons in 1967-68, which more than doubled to 79.5 thousand metric tons in 1972-73 [cite: 2242]. The yield was 612 kg per ha as against 421 kg of the previous year [cite: 2243]. Tuar and moong are frequently grown in a mixture with jowar [cite: 2244]. Teora and masur are also grown as kharif crops much under the same conditions as gram [cite: 2245].

#### Jowar (Sorghum vulgare)
Gram is followed by jowar, which is the third important crop of the District and the most important kharif crop [cite: 2248, 2249]. It occupied 82,840 ha in 1967-68, working out to 17 per cent of the cropped area [cite: 2249]. The popularity of jowar has grown enormously, as clearly borne out from the statistics of jowar area in 1972-73 [cite: 2250]. The area had gradually declined to 4.91 thousand ha *[sic]* [cite: 2251]. About the previous decreasing trend in jowar area, Dr. Mukerjee in his Report says:

> *"Even in the Kharif crop, jowar which is the most important in Bhilsa shows a diminution from 1,40,625 acres in 1929-30 to 1,15,761 acres in 1943-44... The main cause of diminution of area under jowar in Bhilsa is high rainfall accompanied by the bad distribution of rain. Neither rice nor maize is grown to any appreciable extent."* [cite: 2251]

But the picture is different now [cite: 2251]. From 35.7 thousand metric tons in 1966-67 to 61.6 thousand metric tons in 1967-68, jowar recorded a bumper outturn [cite: 2252]. However, there was a falling off in production when it amounted to 39.3 thousand metric tons in 1972-73 [cite: 2253]. The yield in the year 1971-72 was 398 kg per ha, though it was 536 kg in the previous year [cite: 2254].

#### Maize (Zea Maya)
Maize is a cereal of lesser importance [cite: 2255]. Amongst the kharif crops of the District, maize comes second only to jowar, occupying 7,812 ha (or 1.6 per cent of the crop area) in 1967-68 and eight thousand ha in 1972-73 [cite: 2255]. With 16,426 acres in 1956-57 and 16,851 acres in 1964-65, the area was almost constant [cite: 2256]. Production of maize was 8.9 thousand metric tons in 1967-68 and 9.9 thousand metric tons in 1972-73 [cite: 2256, 2257]. The current yield was 974 kg per ha in 1971-72 [cite: 2259].

#### Rice (Oryza sativa)
Paddy has little relevance to Vidisha District [cite: 2261]. Its area appears to be in continuous decline, being no more than 1.7 thousand ha in 1972-73 [cite: 2261]. About the desirability of cultivating these three kharif crops (jowar, maize, rice), the old Report states:

> *"Laboratory experiments in rice, maize and jowar may give us the suitable varieties that may be tried with a view to the improvement of kharif area and yields. Their cultivation is important from another point of view. It is jowar, rice and maize that supply the stalks used as cattle fodder, the lack of which has led to the marked deterioration of cattle in that district."* [cite: 2262]

#### Fruits and Vegetables
Of late, a taste and liking for fruits and vegetables have developed to some extent, as evident from the increase in their area from 1,452 acres in 1956-57 to 2,886 acres in 1964-65 [cite: 2263]. It was, however, 1,043 ha in 1971-72, out of which vegetables occupied 500 ha and fruits 543 ha [cite: 2264]. The most important fruit crop of the District is mango with 329 ha, followed by guava with 98 ha [cite: 2265, 2266]. A Vegetable Development Scheme was initiated in the year 1967-68 [cite: 2266]. New areas have been brought under vegetables, including potato (50 ha), sweet potato (20 ha), onions (100 ha), and common country vegetables [cite: 2267].

#### Condiments and Spices
Condiments and spices slowly increased in area both before and after 1955-56, when Sironj was included in the District [cite: 2268]. From 737 acres in 1950-51, they increased to 818 acres in 1954-55 and then to 1,217 acres in 1955-56, from where they rose slowly and erratically to 1,458 acres in 1960-61 [cite: 2269]. Latest figures show that the increase in area has continued, covering 1,725 acres in 1964-65 [cite: 2270, 2271]. In 1971-72, the area under these crops was 535 ha [cite: 2271]. Chillies covered the largest area with 345 ha, followed by coriander (*dhania*) (115 ha), garlic (*lahsun*) (42 ha), and ginger (*adrak*) (18 ha) [cite: 2272].

#### Oil Seeds
The area under oilseeds has been uncertain and variable throughout [cite: 2273]. It reached a level of 80,347 acres in 1964-65, the highest since 1950-51 [cite: 2273]. In 1967-68, oilseeds crops accounted for 37,134 ha, covering 7.7 per cent of the total cropped area [cite: 2274]. The year 1971-72 reported 34.3 thousand ha under oilseeds [cite: 2275].

Linseed (*Linum usitatissimum*), the chief oilseed crop, accounted for 42 per cent of the oilseed crop area in 1967-68 [cite: 2277]. In 1967-68, linseed covered 15,702 ha, with a quinquennial average of 14,168 ha [cite: 2278]. Almost the entire crop is raised by the drilling method [cite: 2279]. In 1972-73, the area increased to 20.3 thousand ha [cite: 2279].

Sesamum or *til*, grown on fairly light soils in the kharif season, is an annual herbaceous plant [cite: 2283]. Amongst oilseeds, it is the second most important crop [cite: 2284]. Its cultivation has assumed importance; in 1967-68 it commanded 9,994 ha, against a quinquennial average of 5,414 ha [cite: 2284]. In 1972-73, the area shrunk to 1.2 thousand ha [cite: 2285]. Another oilseed crop commanding a sizeable area was rape and mustard (4.8 thousand ha) in 1972-73 [cite: 2286].

#### Fibres
Fibres are not important in the agricultural economy of Vidisha [cite: 2287]. In 1967-68, the area under fibres was 789 ha [cite: 2287]. Of this, the largest area, 448 ha, was occupied by ambadi (*mesta*) [cite: 2288]. Cotton occupied the least area with only 33 ha [cite: 2288].

#### Drugs and Narcotics
Among drugs and narcotics, the only one produced on any significant scale was tobacco, with 17 ha in 1967-68, which is much lower than historical levels [cite: 2290]. In 1951-52 it covered 192 acres, while back in 1906-07, when it was eminently conspicuous in trade, it covered an area of 1,326 acres in the then District of Bhilsa [cite: 2291]. In fact, Bhilsa tobacco was famous all over the country [cite: 2292]. Edward Thornton, writing in the middle of the 19th century, considered Bhilsa tobacco produced in the vicinity of the town to be the finest in India [cite: 2293].

### Progress of Scientific Agriculture

#### Agricultural Machinery & Implements
Vidisha District has a comparatively greater number of large-size holdings and therefore offers better scope for mechanized agriculture [cite: 2295, 2296]. Consequently, Vidisha has many more tractors on its fields than most districts of Madhya Pradesh [cite: 2297]. Simple farm machine tools are also manufactured locally [cite: 2298, 2300].

Tractors multiplied considerably during the inter-census decades (1951-61), covering the first two Five Year Plans [cite: 2301]. From a meager total of 4 tractors in 1950-51, their number rose to 31 in 1956, 142 in 1961 (with 121 privately owned), 143 in 1967-68, and swelled to 404 by 1971-72 [cite: 2302, 2303]. This large share of private ownership reflects the progressive outlook and relative affluence of the cultivators [cite: 2307].

However, traditional implements remain highly in vogue [cite: 2308]. The age-old *hal* (country wooden plough with a spike iron share penetrating 6 inches) and the *bakhar* (paring plough with a horizontal blade used to clear weeds and prepare land) still play dominant roles [cite: 2309, 2310, 2312]. Other traditional tools include the *dora* (for weeding), *nai* (bamboo tube seed-drill fixed behind the plough share), *duffan* (a *nai* with two tubes), *gaintee*, *phaora* (spade), *khurpi* (hoe), and *duranta* (sickle) [cite: 2313, 2314, 2315].

These are being supplemented by new implements such as chaff-cutters, power and bullock-driven cane-crushers, Gwalior ploughs, improved *duffans* and *tiffans*, winnowers (Vidisha and Hoshangabad made), olpad threshers, Ludhiana threshers (tractor-drawn), and locally made threshing-cum-winnowing machines [cite: 2316, 2317].

Achievements in the distribution of improved implements during the five years (1963-68) were: ploughs 1,688; chaff-cutters 201; winnowers 513; threshers 25; touchi-gurma 97; hoes 64; cultivators 58; seed drills 221; cane-crushers 95; duffans 769; tiffans 250 and tractors 9 [cite: 2319, 2320, 2321].

#### Seeds
Seed improvement work was conducted in the past, but results were largely confined to experimental fields [cite: 2324, 2328]. Post-Independence planning brought concerted efforts to distribute high-yielding strains for wheat, gram, paddy, jowar, maize, moong, etc [cite: 2330, 2331, 2332]. By 1967-68, 2,40,000 acres of rabi and 80,000 acres of kharif land had been brought under improved seeds [cite: 2333]. A high-yielding variety programme was initiated in 1968-69 [cite: 2344].

#### Manures and Fertilizers
Chemical fertilizer distribution is heavily dependent on irrigation availability and adequate rainfall [cite: 2353, 2354]. Among nitrogenous fertilizers, ammonium sulphate has been distributed in the largest quantity, followed by urea, C.A.N., and nitrophosphate, while super-phosphate is the principal phosphatic fertilizer [cite: 2362]. Use of potassic fertilizers started later and remains small [cite: 2363].

| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

| Potassic | — | — | — | 0.9.50 | 1.2.50 |

[cite: 2357, 2358]

Traditional organic manures consist of cow-dung and village sweepings [cite: 2367]. Systematic rotting pits have been adopted under Community Development guidance [cite: 2368]. Green manuring with *san*, *dhaincha*, and *moong* has also become popular, especially historical application to poppy fields [cite: 2369, 2370]. It is estimated that 50 per cent of cow-dung is lost to fuel uses due to scarcity of wood in this scantily forested district [cite: 2371, 2372]. Goat and sheep folding on fields is also practiced for fertilization, particularly in Lateri tahsil [cite: 2373, 2374].

Traditional rotation methods include: wheat after gram; paddy after peas; jowar after wheat and gram; and maize after lentil and peas [cite: 2383]. Mixed cropping includes wheat-gram, wheat-gram-linseed, jowar-moong-tur, and jowar-urad [cite: 2384]. Leaving land fallow has drastically declined due to fertilizer inputs and population pressure [cite: 2385].

#### Agricultural Diseases and Pests
Among seed-borne diseases, rust or blight (called *gerua*) affects wheat and jowar, historically causing sudden widespread destruction [cite: 2387, 2390]. Pre-sowing seed dressing with mercurial compounds is an effective preventative [cite: 2391]. Frosts also cause occasional damage [cite: 2391].

Pests include locusts, field/godown rats, storage beetles, and moths [cite: 2392, 2393, 2394]. Major weeds infesting crops include *kans*, followed by *govi*, *motha*, *gokhru*, *nathni*, *katai*, *baridudin*, *hirankur*, *bathua*, *nohia*, *agiya*, and *hardila* [cite: 2397]. Neglected fields quickly become overgrown with *kans*, converting fields into culturable waste [cite: 2398].

Under the Plant Protection Scheme from 1963-68, seed treatment covered 6,269 quintals (spanning 2,27,506 acres) [cite: 2401]. Out of 1,83,392 acres affected by pests and diseases, 42,972 acres were controlled, and a large-scale anti-rat operation successfully protected 71,224 acres out of 85,454 affected acres, utilizing 8.49 quintals of rodenticides [cite: 2406, 2407].

From 1963-64 to 1967-68, specific line sowing covered 5,32,233 acres; crop rotation covered 2,80,827 acres; mixed cropping with legumes covered 2,57,719 acres; contour cultivation spanned 26,601 acres; and strip-cropping spanned 22,577 acres [cite: 2412, 2413, 2414].

#### Agricultural Farms
1.  **Vidisha Agricultural Farm:** Established in 1943, covering 140 acres (112 acres cultivated) on the Vidisha-Sanchi road [cite: 2422, 2426, 2427]. Devoted to research and seed multiplication [cite: 2422]. It consists of homogenous typical medium to deep black soil of the Malwa plateau ("dark grey clay without calcareous layer") [cite: 2429, 2430, 2448]. Clay content ranges between 41–55%, silt 14–29%, sand 10–25% [cite: 2432, 2433]. It has high moisture retention but is workable with difficulty (sticky when wet, very hard when dry) [cite: 2435, 2436, 2437]. pH varies from 7.0 to 7.9 (near neutral) [cite: 2440]. Soluble salt content is safe (0.03 to 0.17%) [cite: 2441]. It is poor in nitrogen (0.003 to 0.006%), very low in organic carbon (0.21 to 0.34%), and low-to-medium in available $P_2O_5$ (15 to 40 lbs/acre) [cite: 2444, 2445, 2446]. Crops are 80% rabi (wheat, gram, linseed) and 20% kharif (jowar, pulses) [cite: 2429].
2.  **Kurwai Seed Multiplication Farm:** Set up in 1959-60, covering 100 acres [cite: 2449, 2450].

#### Co-operative Farming
Planned organization of joint farming societies began in 1961-62 [cite: 2453]. Vidisha was one of 20 districts selected for pilot projects, establishing 8 joint agricultural institutions on pooled lands [cite: 2454, 2458]. Total farming cooperatives grew from 6 (end of First Plan) to 8 (end of Second Plan), 19 (end of Third Plan), and 20 in 1966-67 [cite: 2461, 2462, 2463]. Service cooperatives reached a peak of 282 by the close of the Third Plan [cite: 2466].

### Animal Husbandry and Fisheries
Total livestock in 1961 numbered 6,35,026 (including 4,86,468 cattle, 89,530 buffaloes, 38,614 goats, and 12,873 sheep) [cite: 2468, 2469]. By 1967-68, livestock stood at 5,77,725, showing a decline in cattle numbers but an increase in buffaloes, sheep, and goats [cite: 2469, 2470].

The primary local breed is the **Malwi** (medium size, grey or silver-grey color, very strong and active for plough and cart work, wide and deep bodies, hard round feet) [cite: 2470, 2471, 2472]. The cows are noted to be poor milkers [cite: 2473]. Area under fodder crops stood at 44,957 ha in 1967-68 [cite: 2476, 2477]. Silo-pit demonstrations grew from 14 (Second Plan) to 61 (Third Plan) [cite: 2482].

*Shri Gandhi Goshala* was established at Vidisha headquarters in 1956 by the Madhya Bharat Government to upgrade Malwi cows using Hariyana bulls, operating on an 83.5-acre Sewage Farm on a 25-year lease [cite: 2487, 2488, 2489, 2491].

Goat and sheep breeding is largely pursued by poorer sections [cite: 2495]. In Kurwai tahsil, 13 specific shepherd families rear herds of 1,619 sheep, harvesting local wool to weave blankets for their livelihood [cite: 2501, 2504, 2505]. Poultry numbered 26,730 by 1968 [cite: 2508]. A District Poultry Unit was set up in the Third Plan [cite: 2513].

#### Fisheries
Planned fisheries development began after Independence under the Fisheries Sub-Division of Guna district [cite: 2515, 2516]. Rivers yield adequate quantities of carp varieties, but district tanks are highly seasonal and dry up in summer [cite: 2519]. The tank at Gyaraspur is the primary center used for fishery development [cite: 2520]. Strains introduced include: *Catla catla*, *Labeo rohita* (rohu), *Labeo calbasu*, *Cirrhina mrigal*, and *Barbus tor* (mahaseer) [cite: 2522, 2523, 2524]. Fishermen utilize gill nets, cast nets, drag nets, *mahajal*, and improved nylon *phaslajal* (used for 70% of tank extractions) [cite: 2525, 2526]. A Fishermen's Cooperative Society was registered in 1967-68 [cite: 2537, 2538].

| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1963-64 | 0 | 72 |
| 1964-65 | 14 | 4 |
| 1965-66 | 93 | 50 |

[cite: 2534, 2535, 2536]

#### Quality Upgrading & Veterinary Services
Upgrading of local breeds is managed via village breeding stations (at Davar, Mundra, Rengri, and Imelia) and an Artificial Insemination (AI) Centre at Vidisha established during the First Plan [cite: 2544, 2545, 2546]. Key Village Centres expanded drastically from 6 to 25 in 1967-68 under the *Intensive Cattle Development Project* [cite: 2549, 2553, 2554]. Major cattle fairs are held at Vidisha (Ramlila mela), Lateri, Basoda, and Sironj [cite: 2558].

Common animal diseases include rinderpest (*mata*), haemorrhagic septicaemia, and foot-and-mouth disease (*khasith* or *roga*) [cite: 2561, 2562]. Traditional indigenous remedies historically included "firing" for conditions like *chakkarpani* (seizures) and *leda* (neck swellings), or calling priests, though these are going out of use [cite: 2563, 2564].

Historically, five veterinary hospitals existed (Bhilsa, Basoda, Kurwai, Shamsabad, and Bhonrasa) [cite: 2566]. Under the Plans, these expanded to 5 hospitals and 20 dispensaries [cite: 2569]. Inoculations against rinderpest rose to 71,332 in 1967-68 [cite: 2572]. Total animals treated reached 42,355 in 1967-68, alongside 3,023 castrations [cite: 2573, 2574].

### Forestry
Vidisha has limited rich forest growth, total forest area standing at 80,677 hectares, primarily situated in the Sironj Sub-division and on the eastern mountainous ranges [cite: 2580, 2581]. Light loam on slopes yields good teak [cite: 2583]. Sixty per cent of forest produce is timber and fuel wood [cite: 2585].

Minor produce includes bamboo, gum, tendu leaves (used for bidi cottage industries), lac (used for making bangles in Basoda tahsil), honey, wax, and catechu (*katha*) [cite: 2586, 2589]. Special forest grasses include *gondra* (found in Kurwai, used for weaving mats) and date palm leaves [cite: 2587, 2588].

### State Assistance to Agriculture
Taccavi loans are provided under the Land Improvement Loans Act of 1883 (for bunding, wells) and the Agriculturists Loans Act of 1884 (for bullocks, seeds, fertilizers), plus the Grow More Food Scheme [cite: 2605, 2606]. Total distributed taccavi loans grew from Rs. 98,530 (1951-52) to Rs. 2,00,628 (1960-61), peaking at over three lakhs in 1959-60 [cite: 2607, 2608]. Loans now cover tractor purchases, insecticides, and plant protection [cite: 2609].

### Famine and Floods
Famine and severe scarcity are generally rare in the fertile Malwa tract [cite: 2611, 2612, 2613]. An exception occurred during the severe drought and famine of 1899-1900 (*Chappan ka sal*, V.S. 1956), when rainfall dropped to 9 inches instead of 30, causing high mortality and empty village houses [cite: 2615, 2619, 2620]. Modern communication and transport lines have eliminated famine risk [cite: 2621].

To control water volume risks, two major flood protection schemes have been initiated: the **Nagwas flood protection scheme** (Kurwai tahsil) and the **Rawan flood protection scheme** (Basoda tahsil), constructing protective embankments [cite: 2624, 2625].

---

## CHAPTER V: INDUSTRIES

### Population Dependent on Industries
In 1961, 22,670 workers (10% of total workers) were dependent on industries [cite: 2630]. Of these, 11,370 worked in household industries, 8,237 in agro-allied activities (forestry, livestock), and 3,063 in manufacturing other than household industries [cite: 2631, 2632].

| Vidisha | 2,360 | 3,812 | 1,072 | 7,244 |
| Basoda | 2,703 | 3,446 | 869 | 7,018 |
| Kurwai | 1,042 | 963 | 196 | 2,201 |
| Sironj | 1,750 | 2,054 | 829 | 4,633 |
| Lateri | 382 | 1,095 | 97 | 1,574 |
| **Total** | **8,237** | **11,370** | **3,063** | **22,670** |

[cite: 2634, 2635, 2636]

According to the 1971 Census, industrial workers stood at 9,551 (339 in mining, 5,442 in household industry, and 3,730 in manufacturing) [cite: 2646, 2648]. The steep decline from 1961 is assigned to a stricter change in the definition of a 'worker' in 1971 [cite: 2649, 2650].

### Old Time Industries
Traditional crafts include handloom weaving, cloth dyeing/printing, carpentry, blacksmithy, shoe-making, lac-bangles, and wool rug making [cite: 2652]. The floor-cloths (*jajams*) of Basoda and the red sandstone containers (*kundies*) of Udaypura hold great local reputation [cite: 2654]. Extensive sandstone and limestone masonry works are documented since early periods in Bhilsa and Gyaraspur [cite: 2655, 2556].

#### Sironj Textile and Craft History
Sironj was at its peak of commercial prosperity during Akbar's reign, acting as a prominent trading station on the highway from Delhi to the Deccan [cite: 2659]. It was famous for producing excellent calicoes and fine printed textiles, as recorded in the *Ain-i-Akbari* and by the 17th-century French traveler Jean Baptiste Tavernier [cite: 2660]. Tavernier notes:

> *"There is made at Sironj, a description of muslin, which is so fine that when it is on the person you see all the skin as though it were uncovered. The merchants are not allowed to export it and the governor sends all of it for the great Mughal's seraglio and for the principal courtiers..."* [cite: 2670]

Sironj produced a heavily exported colored cloth called **chintz**, popular in Persia and Turkey for bed-covers and garments [cite: 2671, 2672, 2676]. The exceptional brilliance of Sironj dyes was locally attributed to the properties of its river water [cite: 2674, 2675]. Large military tents were also a Sironj specialty manufactured for Maratha armies [cite: 2678].

Another historic industry was **paper manufacturing** at the *Kagajipura* locality along the river; the thick, durable Sironj paper was used for centuries-old official *sanads* [cite: 2697, 2698, 2700]. These industries decayed by 1910 due to competition from foreign factory-made chemical dyes and imported papers [cite: 2700, 2701]. Today, Sironj remains known for durable shoes, blankets, *sarotas* (betel-nut clippers), and shaving razors crafted by local blacksmiths [cite: 2701, 2702, 2821].

### Power
Electricity infrastructure was historically limited [cite: 2706]. Power generation began in 1930 when the *Madhya Bharat Electric Supply Co.* started a 27 kW single diesel generator set at Kurwai [cite: 2707, 2708]. In 1944, the *Malwa Electric Supply Co.* launched a 70 kW station at Vidisha town with two 35 kW sets [cite: 2708, 2709].

Diesel stations operated at Kurwai, Vidisha, Sironj, and Basoda until 1967-68, when they were closed down in favor of bulk hydro-electric supply from the **Chambal Project** starting in 1968-69 [cite: 2719, 2720, 2721]. The Bhopal Electrical Division was formed in 1970 [cite: 2729].

Electrified pumps grew from 164 (1969) to 267 (1971) and 660 by 1974 [cite: 2733]. Total electrified villages grew from 3 in 1961 to 24 in 1968-69, and 74 by the end of 1974 [cite: 2737, 2739].

### Mining and Manufactures
Mines and quarries extract minor minerals like clay, sandstone, murram, and *chhuimitti* [cite: 2749].

#### Large and Medium Scale Industries
There is no heavy industry in Vidisha District [cite: 2756]. Five major factories were registered under the Factories Act in 1969 [cite: 2756].
1.  **Madhya Pradesh Lamps Industry, Vidisha:** Under construction on a 10-hectare plot on Sanchi Road by the MP State Industries Corporation in joint sector collaboration with Bajaj Electricals [cite: 2757, 2758, 2763]. Capital investment of Rs. 2.5 crores; expected to produce bulbs and tubelights worth Rs. 3 crores annually starting in 1976, employing 300 workers [cite: 2763, 2764].
2.  **Madhya Pradesh Board and Paper Mills, Vidisha:** Licensed in November 1963; began production in June 1966 manufacturing straw board and mill board pulp with an annual capacity of 2,880 tonnes [cite: 2767, 2769]. Total capital investment was Rs. 16 lakhs (Rs. 13.50 lakh fixed, Rs. 2.50 lakh working capital) [cite: 2770, 2771].

Total investment by 1969 was Rs. 25 lakhs (Rs. 10 lakh fixed, Rs. 15 lakh working), employing 250 workers [cite: 2781, 2782, 2783].

#### Small Scale Industries
Small scale units expanded across successive plans: 1 unit existed pre-Independence, 13 were added in the Second Plan, 14 in the Third Plan, and 23 during 1967-69 [cite: 2787, 2788, 2789, 2790]. By December 1969, there were 55 registered small scale industries (employing 5–9 workers with power, or 5–19 without power) and 893 minor unregistered workshops employing fewer than 5 workers [cite: 2791, 2792].

#### Industrial Co-operative Societies & State Support
The state grants power consumption subsidies and managerial aid to industrial cooperatives [cite: 2846, 2847]. Financial assistance grew from Rs. 11,000 (First Plan) to Rs. 1,30,165 (Second Plan) and Rs. 2,27,665 (Third Plan) [cite: 2848, 2849, 2850, 2853].

The **MP Khadi Gramodyog Parishad** advanced loans and subsidies across the plans [cite: 2858]. During the Second and Third Plans combined, total loans advanced were Rs. 2,43,087 to 38 cooperative societies and 1 individual unit, along with Rs. 41,731 in subsidies [cite: 2863, 2865, 2866, 2869, 2870].

The **MP Financial Corporation** sanctioned loans of Rs. 1 lakh (Second Plan), Rs. 5.77 lakhs (Third Plan), and in 1969-70 sanctioned Rs. 3.94 lakhs to 6 small scale units and Rs. 8 lakhs to two larger business concerns [cite: 2871, 2872, 2873, 2874, 2876]. An **Industrial Estate** was established on 6th December, 1963 near Teonda Sagar Road at Vidisha, providing six factory sheds ($120' 	imes 60'$) on rent [cite: 2877]. A Rural Workshed also operates at Kurwai [cite: 2878].

#### Labor Welfare and Minimum Wages
Nine labor and employers' organizations were historically registered up to 1969 (e.g., *Rashtriya Congress Mazdoor Sangh* at Ganj Basoda, *Vidisha Nagarpalika Karamchari Sangh*, *Bidi Mazdoor Union* at Sironj, *Vidisha Sahkari Adikosh Employees Sangh*) [cite: 2913, 2914, 2916, 2918].

Welfare is strictly governed on a statutory basis via the **Factories Act of 1948** (mandating dining/rest sheds, clean water, machine safety, creches, and canteens) [cite: 2922, 2923, 2925, 2926]. Under the **MP Fixation of Minimum Wages Act of 1962**, minimum wages are enforced for workers in scheduled processing plants (flour, pulses, rice, oil mills, bidi making, stone crushing, and public motor transport) [cite: 2931, 2935, 2937].

The **Employees' Provident Fund Act of 1952** covered six establishments by 1972, supporting 665 subscribers (led by Standard Flour with 314, MPSRTC with 159, and Central Cooperative Bank with 120) [cite: 2943, 2945, 2948, 2949].

---

## CHAPTER VI: BANKING, TRADE AND COMMERCE

### History of Indigenous Banking
During the medieval period, prosperous indigenous banking houses called *Kothee-walas* operated at Sironj, controlling local commerce through the extensive use of bills of exchange (*hundies*) [cite: 2956, 2958, 2959, 2961]. Tavernier documents an exchange house at Sironj offering letters of credit for Surat at a highly competitive discount rate of 3 per cent (compared to rates up to 6.25 per cent in other Indian trade centers) [cite: 2960, 2961, 2962].

At the close of the 17th century, nomadic *Banjaras* managed bulk transport, while Jain and Vaishnav *Baniyas* controlled mercantile credit [cite: 2963, 2964, 2965]. By the 19th century, the Maheshwari community emerged as exceptionally wealthy financiers [cite: 2967]. Unorganized professional money-lenders (*sahukars*) continue to hold a prominent position, supplying around two-thirds of rural credit requirements [cite: 2975, 2976, 2977].

### Indebtedness
A sample survey executed by the Reserve Bank of India's *All India Rural Credit Survey Committee* between November 1951 and July 1952 detailed heavy agricultural debt [cite: 2991, 2992]. The average debt per family in Vidisha District was Rs. 362 (cultivators Rs. 531, non-cultivators Rs. 125) [cite: 2992, 2993]. The average debt per indebted family was Rs. 476 (cultivators Rs. 695, non-cultivators Rs. 186) [cite: 2994, 2997]. The proportion of indebted families stood at 76 per cent (82.3% for cultivators) [cite: 2998].

[cite: 3001, 3002, 3003]

Borrowings were largely allocated to capital expenditures on farms (46.6% or Rs. 125) and family expenditures (39.6% or Rs. 107) [cite: 3010, 3011]. Unscrupulous lenders traditionally charged interest rates as high as 24 per cent, trapping families in perpetual debt [cite: 3069, 3070, 3072].

### Co-operative Banking

#### 1. The Vidisha Central Co-operative Bank Ltd., Vidisha
The movement launched on 21st January, 1916 as the non-registered *Madhav District Central Co-operative Bank, Bhilsa*, later registered under the new Co-operative Act on 19th July, 1918 [cite: 3094, 3105, 3106, 3107]. Authorised share capital was Rs. 2,00,000 [cite: 3108]. Branches opened over time: Ganj-Basoda (1951), Sironj (1957), Lateri (1961), Shamshabad (1962), Gyaraspur (1964), Gulabganj (1965), Teonda (1970), and Nateran (1971) [cite: 3109, 3110]. In 1969-70, paid-up capital stood at Rs. 32,40,850, working capital at Rs. 2,77,646, deposits at Rs. 69,72,757, and loans received from outside institutions reached Rs. 1,50,40,081 [cite: 3111, 3112, 3113, 3114]. Total loans advanced to member societies in 1969-70 were Rs. 2,24,00,789 [cite: 3115, 3116].

#### 3. Primary Credit Societies
Provide short-term crop loans and distribute fertilizers on agency terms [cite: 3156, 3157]. Reorganization measures after the Third Plan systematically consolidated unviable units, reducing active societies from 677 (1960-61) to 194 (1966-67), and down to 173 agricultural societies by 1970-71 [cite: 3165, 3170, 3171].

### Joint Stock Banks
Commercial banking commenced in 1935 when the *Imperial Bank of India* launched a branch at Ganj Basoda, followed by Vidisha in 1938 [cite: 3194, 3195]. *Bharat Bank, Delhi* operated a branch at Vidisha from 1945 until its 1952 amalgamation with the *Punjab National Bank* [cite: 3196]. Following the 1948 creation of Madhya Bharat, PNB and *United Commercial Bank* opened branches [cite: 3197]. The *State Bank of Indore* started its Ganj Basoda branch on 18th August, 1961 [cite: 3201, 3202].

Following the August 1969 bank nationalisation, total commercial bank offices rose to 7 by September 1970 (State Bank of Indore operating branches at Ganj Basoda, Lateri, Kurwai, Vidisha, and a pay office at Sironj; plus PNB and UCO Bank operating single branches at Vidisha) [cite: 3203, 3204, 3205, 3207, 3211]. Average population served per banking branch dropped from 1,42,000 (1967) to 88,000 (1970) [cite: 3213, 3214]. Total commercial bank deposits in the District reached Rs. 104 lakhs by 1970 [cite: 3217, 3218].

The **Lead Bank Scheme** was initiated late in 1969, with the *State Bank of Indore* designated as the Lead Bank for Vidisha District to identify credit gaps and expand institutional credit to weaker sections [cite: 3232, 3233, 3234].

### Small Saving Schemes & Insurance
Available programs include Post Office Savings Banks, 12-Year National Plan Certificates, 10-Year Treasury Savings Deposits, 15-Year Annuity Certificates, and Cumulative Time Deposits [cite: 3253]. Total small savings deposits rose significantly, yielding gross collections of Rs. 11.59 lakhs and net collections of Rs. 5.38 lakhs against a target of 8 lakhs in 1970-71 [cite: 3261].

Life Insurance Corporation operations are managed locally by six Development Officers and 76 agents [cite: 3267]. Total sum assured rose from Rs. 32.12 lakhs across 784 active policies (1963-64) to Rs. 41.75 lakhs across 644 policies (1967-68) [cite: 3271]. A *Salary Saving Scheme* operates inside major educational and industrial units [cite: 3276].

### Currency and Coinage
Historically, Mughal mints struck coins at Ujjain, Bhilsa, Gwalior, and Narwar [cite: 3285]. Following the decline of the Mughals, Jankoji Rao issued Maratha silver coins directly out of a mint located at **Ganj Basoda** [cite: 3286, 3288].

Under Akbar, the **Sironj Mint** held the 23rd rank among 28 official imperial mints, striking copper coins [cite: 3298, 3299]. Sironj rulers like Nawab Baziruddola (1834-64) struck local coins weighing 11 *mashas*, which remained in currency until 1902 [cite: 3299, 3300].

#### Table: Weights of Pre-British Rupee Coins
| Coin Type | Weight in Grains | Pure Silver in 100 Parts | Pure Contents in 100 Grams |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Bhilsa Rupee | 169.62 | 86.5 | 146.65 |
| Sironj Rupee | 168.35 | 84.8 | 142.75 |

[cite: 3303]

The decimal system of currency was uniformly enforced across the District in 1957 [cite: 3304].

### Trade and Commerce
During Akbar's era, Sironj was officially called a *bandar* (prominent trading port/city) along the commercial route connecting Delhi to the south [cite: 3308, 3311]. Sironj market displays a unique structural layout: **two-storeyed shops** built specifically to accommodate customer traffic coming on horses and camels (the upper level served riders directly, while the ground floor served pedestrians), which still exist [cite: 3330, 3331, 3332]. With the late 19th-century construction of the Great Indian Peninsula Railway, trade points shifted toward rail junctions, causing Vidisha and Ganj Basoda to emerge as the primary wholesale markets [cite: 3334, 3335].

The total number of persons engaged in trade and commerce in 1961 was 6,884 (4,239 urban, 2,645 rural) [cite: 3346]. Vidisha tahsil led with 2,704 merchants, followed by Ganj Basoda (1,956) and Sironj (1,434) [cite: 3350]. A Railway Out-agency was opened at Sironj in February 1966, boosting mandi freight exports from 18,292 quintals (1966) to 91,158 quintals (1969) [cite: 3357, 3364].

#### Regulated Mandies and Market Data
Regulated markets exist at Vidisha, Ganj Basoda, Sironj, Shamshabad, Gulabganj, and Kurwai [cite: 3369].

Sironj Mandi (historically known as *Sadatganj*, established March 1938) links local produce directly with major industrial cities across India, exporting grains to Bombay, Calcutta, Madras, Poona, Nagpur, and Ahmedabad [cite: 3382, 3384, 3386, 3387, 3388]. Total outward shipments from Sironj Mandi in 1970-71 included: wheat 41,953 qtls; gram 52,512 qtls; juar 34,116 qtls; and linseed (*alsi*) 20,301 qtls [cite: 3396].

#### Co-operative Marketing Societies
Four principal marketing societies operate (Vidisha, Ganj Basoda, Sironj, and Kurwai) [cite: 3437, 3447]. In 1970-71, their combined capitalization reached Rs. 10.15 lakhs with a working capital of Rs. 36.86 lakhs [cite: 3445]. In 1969-70, aggregate cooperative sales included: agricultural produce worth Rs. 38,05,988; fertilizers for Rs. 95,045; farm implements for Rs. 99,131; and cement/iron sheets for Rs. 2,44,027 [cite: 3457, 3458].

The **District Wholesale Consumers Co-operative Stores, Vidisha** (*Zila Thok Sahakari Upbhokta Bhandar*) was registered on 26th November, 1966, managing retail branches for controlled essential provisions across the four major towns [cite: 3462, 3463, 3464].

#### State Trading and Warehousing
State procurement operates under the *MP Wheat Procurement (Levy) Order of 1965* and *1968*, imposing a 50 per cent levy on licensed dealers [cite: 3486, 3498]. In 1969, the Food Corporation of India procured 2,05,765 quintals of wheat inside the district at standard rates (Rs. 81 for Grade A, Rs. 75 for Grade B, Rs. 74 for Grade C per quintal) [cite: 3506, 3507, 3508]. Controlled distribution runs through 8 statutory fair-price shops [cite: 3516].

The **MP Warehousing Corporation** operates active grain storage terminals at Vidisha, Ganj Basoda, Kurwai, Sironj, and Gulabganj [cite: 3520, 3522]. Cooperative societies receive a special 10 per cent storage fee discount [cite: 3524].

#### Historic Weights and Measures (Gwalior State Systems)
Before standardisation, bulk articles were weighed via native metrics recorded by Captain Luard [cite: 3540]:
* **Mass (Precious Goods):** 8 Khaskhas = 1 Chanwal; 8 Chanwals = 1 Ratti; 8 Rattis = 1 Masha; 12 Mashas = 1 Tola; 5 Tolas = 1 Chhatak [cite: 3542].
* **Mass (Bulk Commodities):** 4 Chhataks = 1 Paua; 2 Pauas = 1 Adhsera (1 lb); 2 Adhseras = 1 Seer; 5 Seers = 1 Panseri; 20 Seers = 1 Kham Maund; 40 Seers = 1 Pakka Maund; 6 Maunds = 1 Mani [cite: 3546]. In villages, grain cups were termed *Barya* (10 chhataks) and *Paiya* (6 seers) [cite: 3566, 3568].
* **Length:** 8 Jau = 1 Ungal; 4 Ungals = 1 Girah; 4 Girahs = 1 Balishta; 2 Balishtas = 1 Hath; 2 Haths = 1 Yard [cite: 3571]. Tailors operated a separate 18-girah yard [cite: 3572]. Sironj traditionally operated the historical *Shahjahanai Gaz* (two girahs longer than an English yard) alongside a 98-tola packa seer up to 1950 [cite: 3604, 3605, 3606].
* **Surface Land Measures:** 3 Gaz = 1 Gatha; 20 Gaz (60 yards) = 1 Jarih chain; 20 Biswas = 1 Bigha; 1,939 Bighas = 1 Acre *[sic]* [cite: 3577, 3584, 3588, 3589, 3590].
* **Time Calculation:** Measured via a floating punctured brass *katori* vessel sinking inside a water pan every exact hour or *ghari* [cite: 3591]. Native table: 60 Bipals = 1 Pal; 60 Pals = 1 Ghari; 2 Gharis = 1 Hour; 2.5 Gharis = 1 Pahar; 7 Pahars = 1 Day and Night [cite: 3594].

Metric units were introduced progressively via the *MP Weights and Measures Enforcement Act of 1959*, making metric mass compulsory on 1st October 1960, metric capacity on 1st May 1962, and metric length on 1st October 1962 [cite: 3608, 3609].

## CHAPTER VII: COMMUNICATIONS

### Old Time Routes
In ancient times, the trade route connecting Paithan in the Deccan to Sravasthi passed directly through the district [cite: 3615, 3616]. In the Muslim period, sections of Sher Shah’s historical *Agra-Burhanpur Road* ran from Handia to Sironj, and onwards to Narwar [cite: 3617, 3618]. The British military *Agra-Bombay Road* was constructed around 1840, traversing the western fringe near Sironj [cite: 3620, 3621].

### Post-Independence Road Networks
By September 1968, the district public works department managed 196.4 km of State Highways, 273.1 km of Major District Roads, and 61.0 km of minor District Roads [cite: 3645, 3646]. The Vidisha-Pachhar Road (97.8 km) and Basoda-Sironj Road were fully black-topped, while others were water-bound macadam [cite: 3648].

Between 1966-67 and 1970-71, new construction covered 195.6 km of roads at an expenditure of Rs. 25.61 lakhs, including major bridge upgrades along the Bina-Kurwai, Kurwai-Shamshabad, and Kurwai-Bamora links [cite: 3659, 3662, 3665].

[cite: 3667, 3668, 3669]

Public motorized transport was launched in 1947 via the *Gwalior North India Road Transport Company*, which was subsequently merged into the **Madhya Pradesh State Road Transport Corporation (MPSRTC)**, managing the bulk of passenger bus routes [cite: 3680, 3682, 3684].

### Railways and Postal History
The district is intersected by 76 kilometers of the **Bhopal-Bina Section** on the Bombay-Delhi main line of the Central Railway, operating stations at Vidisha, Sumer, Gulabganj, Pabai, Ganj-Basoda, Bareth, and Kurwai-Kethora [cite: 3642, 3689]. All mail and express trains halt at Vidisha station [cite: 3690]. There is no air strip; the nearest aerodrome is at Bhopal [cite: 3696].

A regular postal infrastructure emerged following the **Postal Agreements of 1885 and 1888** with the British Government, utilizing Imperial postage stamps surcharged with the words *"Gwalior State"* [cite: 3697, 3698, 3700]. The first combined post and telegraph office opened at Vidisha in 1885 [cite: 3704].

## CHAPTER VIII: MISCELLANEOUS OCCUPATIONS

In 1961, 23,712 workers (10% of total working force) were classified under "other services", which adjusted to 13,705 persons in the 1971 Census due to definitional alterations [cite: 3723, 3725].

### Public Administration and Clerical Base
According to the 1961 Census, public administrative officials and executives numbered 742 (including 11 females) [cite: 3729]. State Government executives numbered 127, while Central Government officials numbered 9 [cite: 3730, 3731]. Village administrative officials formed the bulk with 570 personnel (including 10 females) [cite: 3739].

Clerical employees included: 25 stenographers/typists, 358 bookkeepers, 29 cashiers, and 638 general office clerks [cite: 3743]. Female clerical participation was nearly zero due to deep-seated middle-class social structural restrictions against women entering administrative lines [cite: 3745, 3746].

| Ministerial Officers | 3 | 4 | — | 7 |
| General Establishment | 229 | 34 | 7 | 68 |

[cite: 3735, 3736, 3738]

### Learned Professions
* **Teachers:** Numbered 1,630 (including 188 women) in 1961, with 50 college-level instructors, 114 secondary teachers, and 1,270 middle/primary school teachers [cite: 3760, 3762, 3763]. By 1969-70, active counts stood at 1,224 primary, 379 middle, 192 higher secondary, and 35 college professors, plus 47 engineering college faculty [cite: 3764, 3765].
* **Medical Practitioners:** Covered 119 physicians and surgeons (including 3 females) in 1961 [cite: 3768, 3769]. All 24 allopathic doctors and 2 dentists were strictly concentrated in urban zones [cite: 3771]. In contrast, out of 76 ayurvedic and 5 homeopathic vaidyas, the majority (55 ayurvedic, 2 homeopathic) practiced across rural villages [cite: 3772]. Related medical support staff numbered 272 (comprising 26 registered nurses and 109 midwives/health visitors) [cite: 3773, 3774].
* **Legal Services:** Jurists numbered 98, including 6 judges/magistrates, 69 legal practitioners (lawyers), and 23 petition-writers, completely devoid of female representation [cite: 3781, 3782].
* **Engineers & Architects:** Numbered 62 technical personnel under the PWD executing state construction plans [cite: 3787, 3789].
* **Artists & Writers:** Totaled 445 (including 77 females), heavily dominated by 336 professional musicians, 72 dancers, and 26 sculptors [cite: 3794, 3795, 3796].

### Domestic and Personal Services
* **Tailors and Cutters:** Numbered 1,439 persons (241 females), providing apparel services across local weekly markets [cite: 3805, 3940].
* **Shoe-makers and Leather-cutters:** Totaled 1,276 artisans (including 180 females) [cite: 3811].
* **Blacksmiths:** Numbered 337 workers (including 49 females) [cite: 3813].
* **Carpenters:** Formed a large professional group of 1,826 craftsmen (only 42 females), with 1,547 operating strictly in rural blocks to repair agricultural implements [cite: 3817, 3818].
* **Barbers:** Numbered 1,056 workers, entirely male [cite: 3825].
* **Launderers (*Dhobis*):** Totaled 832 workers; unique because female workers actually outnumbered males, with 456 females active in the trade [cite: 3829].
* **Sweepers and Cleaners:** Numbered 1,580 persons, including 807 females [cite: 3833].
* **Domestic Servants:** Totaled 66 housekeepers and cooks, alongside 226 maidservants [cite: 3837].

Retail trade proprietors numbered 4,706 (with 562 females), operating alongside 347 urban street hawkers and pedlars [cite: 3845, 3848, 3851].

## CHAPTER IX: ECONOMIC TRENDS

### Pattern of Livelihood
Total active workers expanded from 1,13,203 (1951) to 231,250 (1961), driven by territorial mergers and broader census definitions [cite: 3862, 3863, 3864]. Agriculture absorbed the bulk of the population growth, expanding its share of the total livelihood from 72.95% (1951) to 75.22% (1961) [cite: 3865, 3866].

Production other than cultivation employed 22,670 workers in 1961 [cite: 3867]. Trade and commerce attracted 2.96% of the workforce (6,884 persons) [cite: 3872, 3873]. The ratio of non-workers to a single active worker dropped from 1.58 (1951) to 1.11 (1961) [cite: 3890, 3892]. In rural farm tracts, the worker to non-worker ratio was nearly balanced at 1:1.03, whereas urban non-agricultural blocks showed a heavier dependency ratio of 1:1.87 [cite: 3895, 3896].

#### Table No. IX-1: Distribution of Working Population (1971 Census)

| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Total Persons** | **226,749** | **105,933** *(46.72%)* | **83,211** *(36.70%)* | **37,605** *(16.58%)* |
| **Males** | 192,928 | 102,327 *(53.04%)* | 56,570 *(29.32%)* | 34,031 *(17.64%)* |
| **Females** | 33,821 | 3,606 *(10.66%)* | 26,641 *(76.77%)* | 3,574 *(10.57%)* |

[cite: 3905, 3906, 3907, 3908]

The sharp statistical decline in female workers from 30.7% (1961) to 10.96% (1971) was caused directly by structural shifts in the classification definitions of a 'primary worker' [cite: 3903].

### Wholesale Prices

#### Pre-Independence & War Inflation
The 1930s global economic depression caused a severe downward crash in agricultural commodity prices across the Gwalior State [cite: 3920, 3925]. Price deflation persisted right up to the 1939 outbreak of World War II, which induced extreme commodity scarcity and severe price inflation [cite: 3934, 3935].

Following the 1948 creation of the Madhya Bharat Union, price surges caused wheat to skyrocket to 1.5 seers per rupee [cite: 3937, 3938]. The state introduced statutory imports and mandatory levy procurement in January 1949, resetting overseas wheat to 2.5 seers per rupee [cite: 3939, 3940].
