### Diseases Common to the District
As mentioned earlier, cholera, smallpox, malaria, tuberculosis, bowel complaints, etc., are the diseases common to the District. Plague also deserves a mention as a dangerous disease of the past. The general health in the District was affected by these diseases with varying intensity in the past and present.

#### Cholera
Except for making a general remark on the attack of this disease in an epidemic form on many occasions during the last century, there is nothing to show the degree of its virulence each time. During the early years of the present century also cholera had visited the District many times, but district-wise statistics in this regard are, again, not available. In 1900-01 and 1919-20 the entire Gwalior State was in its grip and Vidisha (Bhilsa) was one of the worst affected districts. The preventive measures adopted by the medical department, however, appear to have checked mortality to an extent. In recent years Vidisha District witnessed cholera in an epidemic form in 1951, 1952, 1958, 1960 and 1963 causing 20, 569, 72, 13, and 4 deaths, respectively. The epidemic was at its height in 1952.

The preventive and curative anti-cholera measures generally adopted in the District are, disinfection of the sources of water supply by potassium permanganate, and mass inoculation campaign. At the time of serious outbreaks the medical and public health staff do mass inoculation, impart health education and distribute anti-cholera medicines. Disinfection of vomita, dejects and infected clothes is carried out with the help of local authorities and municipalities. Emergency cholera regulations are also enforced whenever the situation so warrants. The preventive measures are extended to adjacent villages in order to arrest its spreading there. Infectious cases are also treated in the isolation wards of hospitals.

#### Smallpox
It appears from the various administration reports and similar other records that smallpox has not been an infrequent visitor to the District, occasionally assuming the form of an epidemic. But here again, it is difficult to assess the extent of mortality caused by this disease each time for want of data. In 1912-13 smallpox broke out in an epidemic form in certain villages of Vidisha. The mortality caused this time was, though not very high, not negligible either. Available records mention another outbreak of this disease in an epidemic form in 1934-35. In 1950-51 there was a severe outbreak of smallpox at many places in Madhya Bharat, Vidisha being the worst affected District. There were in all 355 attacks and 99 deaths from this disease. In still recent years smallpox visited the District at certain places in an epidemic form almost every year with a gap of one year from 1950-51 to 1968-69. In 1968-69, the number of deaths caused by smallpox was 131.

Vaccination is the only effective preventive measure to stamp out this disease. Until the formation of a separate medical department for Gwalior State in 1887, alongwith which a scheme for regular vaccination was evolved, there existed only dispensary vaccination in the District. Vaccination was carried out by the compounders incharge of the dispensaries also in their immediate vicinity. An attempt, thus, made by the medical department in 1887 to popularise vaccination could not be very successful, for the villagers among whom vaccination was almost a necessity were prejudiced against it. A vaccination manual was compiled in 1910-11. In 1920-21, a depot for the manufacture of vaccine lymph-the only one of its kind then in Central India-was established at Shivpuri. This was followed by the opening of training classes for vaccinators in 1923-24 and the introduction of some legislative measures. Accordingly, the Gwalior vaccination bill was passed into law, taking effect from 30th August, 1942. It was laid down that vaccination and revaccination of children and adults should be resorted to as a measure for personal protection.

In order to root out this disease the National Smallpox Eradication Programme was launched in the District. The number of vaccination, which was only 1,775 in 1951 rose to 11,198 in 1958 and 27,737 in 1970. The details of the vaccination work is given in the Table below.

##### Table No. XVI-4: Vaccination

#### Malaria
The various administration reports relating to the early years of the present century have frequently been reporting that malarial fever was responsible for considerable mortality in this District, though it was not hyperendemic. However, in the absence of relevant data the intensity of its incidence during this period cannot be assessed. However, arrangements appear to have existed for distribution of quinine and other anti-malaria drugs to the needy on nominal rates at medical institutions and through the agency of patwaris, post-offices and Panchayat Boards.

The anti-malarial measures were speeded up after the formation of Madhya Bharat in 1948. Under the National Malaria Control Programme, which was launched in the District in 1953 with its Unit headquarters at Guna, these activities gained further momentum. Two of the sub-units were located in the District at Sironj and Vidisha. The former sub-unit was to cover 2 towns and 722 villages with a population of 2,04,877 while 2 towns and 889 villages with a population of 2,84,336 were to be covered by the latter. The unit activities were confined to spraying of the entire area twice a year with D. D. T. followed by infant and juvenile blood survey to assess the incidence of malaria. In 1959 the National Malaria Eradication Programme replaced the Control Programme. Surveillance work was also started in 1959. The staff for conducting the National Malaria Eradication Programme work in the District included one Assistant Unit Officer, 2 Senior Malaria Inspectors, 2 Junior Malaria Inspectors, 13 Malaria Surveillance Inspectors, 51 Surveillance workers, 1 Field Worker, 2 Drivers and 2 Cleaners.

From 1961 to 1965 there was no DDT spraying as the unit was then under the consolidation phase. Human dwellings and cattlesheds sprayed with DDT in subsequent years numbered 67,76,734 and 10,441 in 1967; 1,01,966 and 15,562 in 1968; and 1,31,034 and 14,810 in 1969, respectively. Children were not examined for spleen rate since the inauguration of NMEP in the District in 1959.

#### Tuberculosis
This disease has been responsible for a sizeable number of deaths in the District every year, though it defied detection in the early years of the present century. In 1957, 1958, 1959, and 1960, there were 33, 405, 205 and 36 deaths, respectively. During the decade 1961-70, the number of deaths caused by T. B. numbered 18, 12, 71 and 115 in 1962, 1963, 1968, and 1969, respectively. For the prevention of T. B., BCG vaccination campaigns are being conducted. To combat this disease there is one T. B. Clinic at Vidisha.

A six bedded (3 male and 3 female) T. B. Clinic was opened at Vidisha in 1955 at the District Hospital. It is staffed by one T. B. Health Visitor (trained at the National Tuberculosis Institute), one Laboratory Assistant (also N. T. I. trained), one nurse, one compounder, two wardboys and one sweeper. The total number of indoor and outdoor patients annually treated here from 1961 to 1968 is given below.

BCG Vaccination campaigns have been launched in the District since 1954. As many as 86,118 persons were tested and 12,478 vaccinated in that year. The BCG team again visited the District in 1956, 1958, 1959, and 1960 vaccinating 6,381, 1,976, 15,033 and 17,958 persons respectively.

#### Eye Diseases
Trachoma and cataract are the two common eye diseases. Under the auspices of the Indian Council of Medical Research, a Pilot Project Survey was conducted in 1959 to assess the incidence of trachoma. The percentage of incidence of trachoma in Vidisha District was estimated to be 39.8. Treatment for these diseases is being offered at the District Hospital Vidisha, and at the civil hospitals, dispensaries and primary health centres.

#### Bowel Complaints
Diarrhoea and dysentery are the two major complaints that cause a high mortality among children. The number of deaths from these diseases has been considerable. During the years 1962 to 1969, the death caused by diarrhoea and dysentery was 993.

#### Plague
As mentioned earlier, the District was completely free from plague during recent years. But it certainly witnessed several virulent attacks in 1903-05, 1913-15, 1917-18 and 1919-20. Owing to the timely precautionary measures adopted, the District has been comparatively free from this dangerous epidemic since then. There has not been an attack of plague in recent years.

### Hospitals and Dispensaries
There were 2 hospitals, 4 dispensaries, 7 primary health centres, 5 maternity and child welfare centres, 10 family planning centres and 21 ayurvedic dispensaries in the District in 1968. The two hospitals are located at Vidisha and Basoda, the former being a District Hospital.

#### District Hospital, Vidisha
Establishment of this hospital can be traced back to the Government dispensary at Vidisha mentioned earlier. This dispensary grew in size gradually and became a hospital. With the formation of a medical district for Vidisha in 1962, this institution began to enjoy the position of a District Hospital. It was then a 40 bedded hospital. Its bed strength was raised to 80 in 1970-Female medical 8, Female Surgical 8, Male medical 20, Male Surgical 20, Isolation 2, Special 4, Maternity Home 12 and T. B. Clinic 6. It is staffed by 12 Doctors. An anti-rabic centre is functioning in this hospital since April, 1962.

#### Civil Hospital, Ganj Basoda
Located at a distance of 2km. from Basoda Railway Station on the main road, the Civil Hospital at Basoda was established in 1929. It had then only its main building and a 4 bedded male ward, besides residential quarters for its staff which consisted of one doctor, one compounder, one nurse and others. A general ward and a maternity ward were added to it in 1956. In 1959 Nurses quarters were also constructed. #### Civil Dispensaries
There are 4 civil dispensaries in the District located at Shamshabad, Gulabganj, Mohammadgarh and Pathari.

Established in 1936 during the Gwalior State regime, the civil dispensary at Shamshabad is located on the west of Shamshabad town. Originally this dispensary had no facilities for indoor treatment. In 1960-61 two small wards for general and maternity cases were constructed. In 1971 it had 8 beds, 4 general and 4 maternity. The dispensary was staffed by one Assistant Medical Officer and other usual staff. The average number of indoor and outdoor patients annually treated here during the period 1961 to 1970 was 2,379 and 26,063 respectively.

Established in 1951, civil dispensary at Gulabganj had only two emergency beds in 1970. An Assistant Medical Officer is in charge of the dispensary. The total number of patients offered treatment here annually during 1966-70 was 16,026; 15,644; 16,268; 14,635 and 15,536 respectively.

The exact date of the opening of civil dispensary Mohammadgarh is not known, but it has been reported to be working since long. Treatment is offered only to out-patients. It is staffed by one Assistant Medical Officer along with other usual staff. The number of patients offered treatment here annually from 1966 to 1969 was 8,937, 7,520, 12,578 and 12,746 respectively.

The Civil Dispensary, Pathari was established in 1911. It has no facility for indoor treatment. It is staffed by one Medical Officer, one compounder, one dresser, two vaccinators, one dai, one wardboy and two others. The total number of outdoor patients annually treated here during 1966-1970 was 22,345, 20,176, 20,408 and 28,417, respectively.

#### Primary Health Centres
With a view to providing increased medical facilities to the rural population several primary health centres were opened in the District during the Plan period. These institutions are located in the Community Development and National Extension Service Block areas. The primary health centres which were established during the intensive phase of the Blocks were taken over by the Public Health Department as and when they entered their post intensive phase. Each primary health centre has three or more sub-centres to extend medical facilities to the interior. Each primary health centre is usually staffed by one Medical Officer, one Sanitary Inspector, one compounder, one dresser, one lady health visitor, four midwives, one wardboy, one peon, one sweeper, one dhobi and one waterman. A midwife is attached to each sub-centre. The Medical Officer incharge of a primary health centre visits each sub-centre once in a week. The staff position, however, varies according to the number of sub-centres and other requirements.

There are seven primary health centres in Vidisha District. Their names, location, year of opening, number of sub-centres and number of beds are given below.

#### Ayurvedic Dispensaries
In pursuance of a resolution passed at a Departmental Conference in 1924 at Shivpuri that the use of indigenous drugs should be freely extended throughout the erstwhile Gwalior State a number of Ayurvedic dispensaries were started during the next four decades. The District had 21 ayurvedic dispensaries run by the Government at the end of March 1968. They were located at Haidergarh, Teyonda, Nateran, Khamtala, Tharr, Bardha, Ahamadpur, Pipaldhar, Udaipur, Massodpur, Khadeh, Bareth, Piklone, Barwai, Mugalsari, Muraria, Bamorishala, Anandpur, Deokhjoori, Khular and Barro. The Ayurvedic dispensary at Bareth is managed by the Tribal Welfare Department. Each dispensary is under a qualified Vaidya. Some of them are provided with a compounder each. Besides these the Municipal Committee, Vidisha, is maintaining an ayurvedic dispensary, staffed by one vaidya, one compounder, one vaccinator and one peon. More than 100 patients are being treated here daily.

#### Medical and Public Health Research Centres
There are no research centres in the District. Dissemination of knowledge of hygiene and public health is, however, offered through the agencies of family planning centres and voluntary organisations like Indian Red Cross Society, Indian Medical Association, etc.

#### Family Planning
In order to provide adequate medical advice to parents, especially to mothers, in bringing up healthy children and in planning their families, a revised and modified family planning programme was chalked out in 1957. Accordingly, a number of urban and rural family planning clinics were opened in quick succession. The various primary health centres in Block areas were the nuclei for family planning programme in rural areas.

For the propagation of family planning programme there is one District Family Planning Bureau at Vidisha, and three urban and six rural clinics located elsewhere in the District. Opened on 1st July 1959, the Bureau is staffed by the District Family Planning Officer, two District Extension Educators (one female), one Field Evaluation Worker, one Block Extension Educator, two Social Workers, one Male Field Worker and one Female Field Worker. #### Indian Red Cross Society
There is a District Branch of the Indian Red Cross Society at Vidisha. The Collector and Civil Surgeon are the President and Secretary of the society, respectively. The society had 51 members on roll in 1968. Its activities include distribution of free medicines, diet, books and magazines among poor and needy patients, dissemination of knowledge of hygiene and public health, and control of epidemics, floods and famines.

#### Indian Medical Association
A District branch of the Indian Medical Association was opened at Vidisha in October, 1961. It had about 20 members on roll in 1968. The Association holds monthly meetings in which topics of clinical interest are discussed. The Association also makes its contribution for flood relief, prevention of epidemics and the propagation of family planning programme.

#### Sanitation
Details regarding the sanitary arrangements that existed in the District earlier are not available. The following passage from the Administration Report of Gwalior State will speak of such facilities provided by the erstwhile State: "To see towards cleanliness and sanitation of villages and to provide at State expense the means of water supply where it was scarce, were, since long, the duties of Pargana Revenue Officers and consequently a considerable number of wells and tanks were constructed by the Revenue and Irrigation Departments for drinking purposes of villagers and their cattle".

The revenue officers were, strictly speaking, only a supervisory staff, for the responsibility actually rested with the Panchayat Boards, constituted in 1912. This being so for rural sanitation, the municipalities were mainly responsible for sanitary arrangements in the urban area.

With the passing of the Madhya Bharat Panchayat Act, 1949 Sanitation in rural areas is being looked after by the respective gram panchayats. However, this responsibility rests with the Blocks in respect of areas under their jurisdiction. The primary health centres established in the block areas are the chief centres of these activities. The medical officer of the primary health centre acts as Health Officer for that area, assisted by the Health Visitor and the Sanitary Inspector. The work done by the seven Blocks in the District, namely, Vidisha, Gyaraspur, Basoda, Nateran, Kurwai, Pathari and Sironj for improving sanitation and conservancy arrangements include the construction of 2164.4 m. of surface drainage, 1871 Latrines, 2135 Soakage pits, and installation of 24 hand pumps during the Second Plan period. During the Third Plan period, 86 hand pumps were installed.

#### Water Supply
Supply of pure drinking water in urban areas is generally the responsibility of the respective municipal committees. However, in Vidisha town pipe water is being supplied by the Public Health Engineering Department. Pipe water supply is not available in any of the other towns in Vidisha District. The town dwellers depend upon water drawn from wells and tanks. And whenever there is scarcity of drinking water, the municipal committee takes necessary steps to make it available to the public through trollies and other means. Kurwai, Ganj Basoda, and Sironj municipal committees are going ahead with water supply schemes.

In the rural areas the gram panchayats and the Development Blocks make arrangements for adequate supply of drinking water by constructing new wells and repairing old ones. Facilities provided by various Blocks in the District in this regard during the Plan period are given below.

##### Table No. XVI-12: Water Supply in Blocks
| Plan Stage | New drinking wells constructed | Existing drinking wells repaired | Stepwells closed |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| I Plan | Nil | Nil | N. A. |
| II Plan | 97 | 77 | N. A. |
| III Plan | 208 | 134 | N. A. |

#### Drainage and Slum Clearance
In Vidisha town this work is being looked after by the Public Health Engineering Department. In Sironj town drains are being cleaned by bhisthis employed by the municipal committee. The municipal committee of Kurwai is contemplating a scheme of drainage for the town. In rural areas this work is the responsibility of the Panchayats and Development Blocks. The work done by the Blocks in respect of surface drainage during the Plan period has already been given under the head 'Sanitation'.

## CHAPTER XVII: OTHER SOCIAL SERVICES

### Prohibition
In principle, the former Madhya Bharat Government was committed to the policy of discouraging use of intoxicants by the people and introducing total prohibition. In practice, however, it followed a cautious policy of implementing prohibition by stages. To begin with, the Madhya Bharat Government introduced total prohibition of liquor, opium and ganja from the 12th December, 1948, in former Bhilsa district including Mohammadgarh, Pathari and Kurwai. Consequently, all the shops selling country liquor (74 in number) and opium and ganja (61 in number) of Bhilsa district, yielding an excise revenue of about Rs. 2,34,500 in 1947-48 were closed.

#### Measures Taken
Provisions were made for supply of these intoxicants to addicts on the basis of medical certificates. The addicts used to get their prescribed quota from Government store-houses. In case of imported foreign liquor and Indian foreign liquor (indigenous liquor bearing the name and to some extent quality of imported liquor) the quota was of six and two bottles, respectively, while in respect of country liquor it was two bottles a month.

Prohibition was extended to Sironj and Lateri tahsils in November, 1956. After reorganisation of Madhya Pradesh, the Central Provinces and Berar Prohibition Act was made applicable in the District. Special staff consisting of one Assistant Prohibition officer, one Inspector, two Sub-Inspectors, two Head Constables and fifteen Constables, was entrusted with the work of prevention, detection and prosecution of offenders under the Prohibition Rules. Subsequently, the special staff was also strengthened by increasing the number of Head Constables to four and that of constables to 40. One Prohibition Propagandist was appointed for Sironj Tahsil also.

With a view to checking illicit distillation of liquor from mahua which grows in abundance in this "dry" area also, Mahua Control Rules were framed and enforced in the District. These Rules were further modified to tighten the provisions relating to possession and sale of mahua for consumption as food and fodder.

# MADHYA PRADESH DISTRICT GAZETTEERS: VIDISHA (PART 2B)

## CHAPTER XVII: OTHER SOCIAL SERVICES (CONTINUED)

### Prohibition Implementation Measures
Following the enforcement of total prohibition on December 12, 1948, across the former Bhilsa district (including Mohammadgarh, Pathari, and Kurwai), the state administration introduced multi-layered oversight bodies [cite: 6115, 6128]. An **Anti-drink Committee** was structured at the District level, featuring the Collector as President, the District Excise Officer as Secretary, and eight non-official members [cite: 6128]. Mirror committees were expanded across tahsil structures and nested inside prominent large villages and towns [cite: 6128].

To conduct systemic counter-intoxicant propaganda, the Government of Madhya Bharat released an official handbook titled *Madya Nishedha* [cite: 6129]. Prohibition Day was celebrated annually on **2nd October** across all administrative circles, deploying public processions, mass assemblies, educational posters, placards, and theater cinema slides [cite: 6130, 6131].

As a tactical safeguard against cross-border smuggling, a **shopless buffer zone** was created by closing all liquor and drug vends within 8 km of the dry border inside adjacent wet districts [cite: 6132, 6133]. This shopless perimeter was extended to a wider **16 km limit** in 1951 [cite: 6133, 6134].

Medical permit applications submitted by addicts were subjected to strict scrutiny to progressively reduce the volume of authorized intoxicants [cite: 6135]. Infractions under the original Madhya Bharat Prohibition Rules carried a penalty fine of Rs. 500 and up to two months of imprisonment [cite: 6138]. This was later tightened under Section 35 of the Excise Act and Section 7 of the Central Provinces and Berar Prohibition Act to **six months' imprisonment, a fine of Rs. 1,000, or both** [cite: 6139, 1640].

### Enforcement Vulnerabilities
Because Bhilsa was the singular district under total prohibition in the former Madhya Bharat territory, it was encircled by wet borders on three sides, complicating enforcement [cite: 6143, 6144]. Addicts crossed borders easily, and non-addicts turned to highly lucrative contraband transit networks [cite: 6145, 6146]. Smugglers frequently operated with motor jeeps or cars and firearms to threaten the under-equipped local excise force [cite: 6147].

Concurrently, **illicit distillation** transformed into a primary source of economic livelihood for clusters of offenders across rural sectors [cite: 6148]. General villagers, intimidated by heavily armed bootleggers and anti-social elements, rarely stepped forward to assist field officers, stripping the department of vital community intelligence and prosecution witnesses [cite: 6153, 6154].

Furthermore, the state force operated without dedicated legal counsel or government prosecutors in the courts; the field Excise Inspector alone handled the complex task of pleading cases and proving technical breaches of the law against experienced defense lawyers engaged by the syndicates [cite: 6155, 6156, 6157]. Legal lacunae routinely allowed violators to secure acquittals [cite: 6158].

The field force was also heavily downsized: by 1962, the localized preventative staff dwindled to **one Head Constable and eight Constables** (compared to a baseline of 4 Head Constables and 40 Constables) [cite: 6159, 6160]. Recognizing these severe structural bottlenecks, the Government of Madhya Pradesh officially **terminated prohibition in Vidisha District on 1st September, 1967**, returning the territory to a wet status [cite: 6162].

---

### Advancement of Backward Classes and Tribes
The district balances a diverse demographic of historically marginalized communities, formally tracking **20 Scheduled Castes** and **7 Scheduled Tribes** under the *Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes Lists (Modification) Order, 1956* [cite: 6164, 6165]. Per the 1961 Census, Scheduled Castes numbered 1,02,352 (21% of the total population) and Scheduled Tribes numbered 21,223 (4% of total records) [cite: 6167]. Their populations are overwhelmingly concentrated in rural zones (91.91% of SCs and 99.31% of STs) [cite: 6168].

#### Civil and Social Rights
Historically subjected to strict untouchability limits, a foundational social breakthrough occurred in **1945** when the ruler of Gwalior State issued an imperial decree throwing open all state-managed temples, public wells, schools, and civic utilities to Harijans on equal terms [cite: 6171, 6175, 6176]. This was supported by nominating representation slots on the state *Praja Sabha*, District Boards, and Pargana Boards [cite: 6177].

Statutory enforcement followed via the *Harijan Disability Removal Act of 1947*, later replaced by the broader *Madhya Bharat Harijan Social Disabilities Removal Act of 1948-49*, which criminalized discrimination across public jobs, trades, businesses, and entertainment venues [cite: 6178, 6179, 6180].

The government established three **Jeevan Vikas Kendras** directed by specialized Harijan Sewaks to spread field propaganda against caste biases, spending Rs. 12,905 on these centers during the First Plan [cite: 6181, 6182, 6183]. Concurrently, the *Zila Dalit Varga Sangh* of Bhilsa drew a direct state grant of Rs. 10,000 [cite: 6184]. On 1st June, 1955, the federal **Untouchability (Offences) Act, 1955** was applied across all blocks, fully penalizing caste discrimination [cite: 6185].

#### Economic Upliftment and Land Tenure
Occupational records from 1961 revealed that 56% of these groups were active workers (55% for SCs, 60% for STs), with **76 per cent of them chained directly to agriculture** (39% as small cultivators, 37% as manual field laborers) [cite: 6188, 6189, 6190, 6191]. Household industries and other manual services accounted for 12% each [cite: 6192, 6195].

Among rural SC cultivating households, 75% held land directly from the state, 10% leased from private landholders, and 15% held mixed land tenures [cite: 6197, 6198, 6199]. Among rural ST households, 82% held state/personal lands, 10% leased from private parties, and 8% held mixed lines [cite: 6201, 6202, 6203].

However, individual surveys showed that the vast majority of these families held small plots insufficient for basic subsistence survival [cite: 6204].

#### State Allotments & Subsidies
To correct this structural imbalance, the state executed targeted land distributions [cite: 6211, 6212, 6213].

[cite: 2615, 6216]

During the Third Plan, 39 SC families received an aggregate of Rs. 1,700 as agricultural subsidy, followed by a major allocation of Rs. 87,250 disbursed across 51 Harijan and Adivasi families in 1967-68 [cite: 6217, 6218, 6219].

#### Vocational Training & State Loans
Under the *Rural Arts and Crafts Scheme*, specialized instructional centers were deployed [cite: 6220]:
* **Leather Goods Training Centres:** Opened at Vidisha (September 1959), training 32 SC candidates up to 1912; Sironj (September 1960), training 14 candidates at a cost of Rs. 12,785; Kurwai (1911), training 19 candidates at a cost of Rs. 14,600; and further wings at Gyaraspur (1964, 17 trainees, Rs. 4,633) and Basoda (1963-66, 23 trainees, Rs. 23,000) [cite: 6221, 6222, 6225, 6227, 6228, 6231, 6232].
* **Ladies Tailoring Centres:** Functional at Vidisha (1911-62, expending Rs. 2,900) and Nateran (1965-66, combined with leather training at a cost of Rs. 10,000) [cite: 6226, 6232, 6233].

During 1967-69, an additional Rs. 11,400 was distributed to 35 individuals for leather works, supplemented by Rs. 5,000 in small-scale industry advances given to 21 SC families [cite: 6240, 6241]. Needy individuals also drew Rs. 1,115 in state legal aid [cite: 6243, 6244].

By 1970, collective farming cooperatives organized for Harijans, Adiwasis, and backward groups reached **34 active societies**, starting with small baseline share capitals between Rs. 130 and Rs. 750 [cite: 6245, 6247, 6250, 6251].

#### Basic Amenities & Public Protections
* **Drinking Water Wells:** Sinking and masonry spending expanded across the decades. The First Plan built two SC and two ST wells (costing Rs. 1,550 and Rs. 4,000) [cite: 6253, 6254]. The Second Plan spent Rs. 52,218 on 28 Harijan wells and Rs. 1,558 on an Adivasi well [cite: 6254, 6255]. The Third Plan allocated Rs. 64,000 for 32 Harijan wells and Rs. 8,500 for 4 Adivasi wells, followed by a Rs. 2,500 well built in 1967-68 [cite: 6256, 6257, 6258].
* **Housing Colonies:** Four large planned model settlements were built at a cumulative cost of Rs. 1,38,100 to house 110 families: three for Scheduled Tribes (at Bareth, Bhilaya, and Amrit-kam) and one for Vimukta Jatis (ex-criminal tribes) at Bhilaya [cite: 6260, 6261, 6262]. The Tribal Welfare Department also runs a dedicated rural Ayurvedic Dispensary at Bareth (active since 1957) [cite: 6264].
* **Public Service Recruitment Enforcements:** Per Government Notification No. 6678-CR-740-1 (August 1958), strict recruitment reservations apply for direct entry: **15% in Class I and II; 16% in Class III; and 20% in Class IV posts** for Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes [cite: 6266, 6267, 6269, 6270, 6271, 6274]. Candidates enjoy a uniform 5-year upper age-limit relaxation and total exemption from application, registration, and exam fees, alongside direct travel allowances for job interviews [cite: 6275, 6276].
* **Municipal Scavenging Reforms:** Under Section 29(iii) of the MP Municipal Act 1961, ward seats are legally reserved for SC/ST representatives [cite: 6278]. Locally, the Vidisha Municipality purchased 72 metal wheelbarrows for Rs. 5,716 to completely ban the degrading practice of carrying night-soil as head-loads, building free housing quarters and spending Rs. 4,000 annually on uniforms for Harijan staff [cite: 6290, 6295, 6296, 6297]. Basoda Municipality distributed 13 hand-carts and 26 storage drums, alongside building 12 quarters leased to sanitary staff at a nominal rate of Rs. 4 per month [cite: 6298, 6299, 6300].

---

### Charitable Public Trusts and Waqfs
All non-Muslim charitable properties are governed under the *Madhya Pradesh Public Trusts Act, 1951*, with the Collector acting as the District Registrar of Public Trusts [cite: 6303, 6304]. All assets must be deposited inside Scheduled Banks or Post Office Savings Banks, and budgets require formal annual approval from the Registrar [cite: 6307, 6308, 6309].

#### Notable Public Trusts
1.  **Shreemant Danaweer Seth Sitab Rai Laxmichand Jain Paramarthik Sansthonka Trust, Vidisha:** Initially registered by Seth Laxmichand in 1935, renewed in 1961 [cite: 6311, 6312]. Current aggregate valuation of its institutional properties is **Rs. 20 lakhs** [cite: 6313]. It directly finances and runs: the SSL Jain Degree College, the SSL Jain Higher Secondary School, the Shri Sheetalanatha Jain Middle School, the Shrimati Saubhagyavati Bai Jain Girls Middle School, the Rajmal Badjatya Jain Shishu Mandir, the Mateshwari Shakkar Bai Jain Scholarship Fund, the Jain Sahityoddharak Fund, a free Charitable Dispensary, a spacious town Dharmashala, and the Shri Shantinatha Swami Digambar Jain Mandir [cite: 6315, 6316, 6319, 6320, 6321, 6322, 6324, 6325, 6326].
2.  **Shri Samantabhadra Jain Paramarthik Trust, Sironj:** Created in 1943-44 by Vaidya Hukumchand Jain with a baseline endowment of Rs. 10,000 [cite: 6327, 6328]. Registered in 1955, it finances the *Digambar Jain Mahilashram* (benefiting 150 female students annually) and the Digambar Jain Boys Hostel [cite: 6329, 6330]. Between 1963 and 1968, it tracked an income of Rs. 6,600 against operational costs of Rs. 5,550 [cite: 6331, 6332, 6333].
3.  **Shri Taran Taran Digambar Jain Trust, Basoda:** Established in 1951 by Seth Ratichand Ramlal Jain, launching a high school that eventually evolved into an Intermediate and Degree College [cite: 6334, 6335, 6336, 6337].

#### Muslim Waqfs
Exempted from the Public Trusts Act, Muslim endowments are governed under the federal *Central Muslim Waqfs Act (XXIX of 1954)* as amended in 1959, falling under the administrative oversight of the **Madhya Pradesh Muslim Waqfs Board, Bhopal** [cite: 6340, 6341]. A total of **273 formal Waqfs** are registered inside the District (9 educational, 131 split religious/charitable, and 133 purely religious) [cite: 6342, 6343].

##### Tahsil-wise Waqf Distribution
* **Sironj Tahsil:** Holds the highest concentration with **156 registered Waqfs** (6 educational, 77 split religious/charitable, and 73 purely religious) [cite: 6343, 6344, 6345].
* **Lateri Tahsil:** Tracks **44 Waqfs** (34 split religious/charitable, 10 purely religious) [cite: 6344, 6348].
* **Vidisha Tahsil:** Tracks **34 Waqfs** (2 educational, 6 split, 26 purely religious) [cite: 6349].
* **Kurwai Tahsil:** Tracks **32 Waqfs** (1 educational, 9 split, 22 purely religious) [cite: 6350].
* **Basoda Tahsil:** Tracks **7 Waqfs** (5 split, 2 purely religious) [cite: 6350].

## CHAPTER XVIII: PUBLIC LIFE AND VOLUNTARY SOCIAL SERVICE ORGANISATIONS

### Representation in the Union Legislature (Lok Sabha)
During the first three federal General Elections (1951-52, 1957, 1962), Vidisha District was mapped entirely within the Single-Member **Guna Lok Sabha Constituency** [cite: 6355].

#### 1. First General Elections (1951-52)
Madhya Bharat State Legislative Assembly representation allocated four seats across three distinct geographic constituencies [cite: 6373, 6374]. The Bhilsa constituency operated as a Two-Member block, reserving one seat for Scheduled Castes [cite: 6375, 6376].

#### 2. Second General Elections (1957)
Following the creation of reconstituted Madhya Pradesh, boundaries shifted into three redefined constituencies, with Vidisha operating as a Two-Member block reserving one seat for Scheduled Castes [cite: 6386, 6387].

In accordance with the statutory *Two-Member Constituencies (Abolition) Act, 1961*, the old composite Vidisha block was split into separate single-seat Vidisha and Basoda blocks [cite: 6395]. ### History of Political Parties Performance

#### Indian National Congress
* **Lok Sabha Performance:** In 1951-52, the party candidate secured 53,549 valid votes in the Guna seat, losing tightly to the Hindu Mahasabha nominee (56,518 votes) [cite: 6422, 6423, 6483]. Congress successfully captured the Guna seat in 1957 (1,18,578 votes) and retained it in 1962 (1,00,135 votes) [cite: 6424, 6425]. However, the party suffered severe losses in the newly structured Vidisha Lok Sabha seat, losing in 1967 to the Jan Sangh (polling 32,820 against Jan Sangh's 1,57,963) and failing again in the 1971 mid-term polls (polling 110,322 against Jan Sangh's 151,386) [cite: 6426, 6427, 6428, 6429, 6521].
* **Vidhan Sabha Performance:** In the 1951-52 debut, Congress captured only the Kurwai seat (6,244 votes), losing the Basoda and Two-Member Bhilsa seats to the Hindu Mahasabha [cite: 6431, 6433, 6434]. However, in subsequent historic bye-elections inside the Bhilsa Two-Member block, Congress swept both seats back with 20,641 and 16,908 votes [cite: 6435, 6436]. In 1957, the party won three seats (Kurwai: 16,692 votes; Vidisha General: 22,543; Vidisha Reserved: 24,753), losing only Sironj to the Mahasabha [cite: 6438, 6439, 6440, 6441]. In 1962, it won Kurwai (14,893) and Basoda (11,413), losing Vidisha by a razor-thin margin of 282 votes [cite: 6444, 6445, 6446]. The 1967 election was a total debacle for Congress; all four candidates collapsed against the Jan Sangh wave [cite: 6448, 6449]. The party staged a partial recovery in 1972, wresting back the Vidisha general seat (23,612 votes) and Sironj (21,192 votes) while yielding Kurwai and Basoda to Jan Sangh [cite: 6451, 6454, 6456, 6457, 6458].

#### Hindu Mahasabha
Wielded immense traditional baseline support across early decades [cite: 6482, 6491]. It successfully captured the Guna Lok Sabha seat in 1951-52 (56,518 votes), though it subsequently lost to Congress in 1957 (polling 58,523) and 1062 (polling 80,409) [cite: 6483, 6484, 6485]. It lost national party status by 1967, and an independent candidate backed by it drew a meager 12,482 votes [cite: 6486, 6487].

In the State Vidhan Sabha, the Mahasabha swept three out of four seats in 1951-52, winning Basoda (7,719 votes) and both seats in the Two-Member Bhilsa block (12,452 and 13,669 votes) [cite: 6491, 6493]. It lost both Bhilsa seats to Congress in the subsequent bye-elections [cite: 6494]. In 1957, it won only Sironj (8,420 votes against Congress's 8,256) [cite: 6438, 6498, 6499]. In 1962, it captured two seats: Sironj (14,623 votes) and Vidisha Reserved (12,483 votes) [cite: 6500, 6501]. Following structural derecognition by 1967, all four independent candidates backed by it lost their security deposits [cite: 6503, 6504].

#### Bharatiya Jan Sangh
* **Lok Sabha Performance:** Debuted in Guna in 1962, polling a minor 20,646 votes [cite: 6518, 6519]. It scored a major victory in 1967, capturing the newly minted Vidisha Lok Sabha seat with a massive landslide of **1,57,953 votes** [cite: 6520, 6521]. In the 1971 mid-term poll, it backed a victorious independent candidate who secured **1,51,386 valid votes** [cite: 6521].
* **Vidhan Sabha Performance:** Absent in 1951-52 and 1957, it recorded minor, losing configurations in 1962 [cite: 6522]. However, in **1967, Jan Sangh completely swept the District, capturing all four Vidhan Sabha seats** (Vidisha: 25,151 votes; Basoda: 29,757; Kurwai: 28,431; Sironj: 19,665) [cite: 6523]. It successfully defended and recaptured the Vidisha seat during a tense 1970 bye-election [cite: 6524]. In 1972, it yielded the Vidisha and Sironj seats to Congress, but successfully retained the Kurwai (29,042 votes) and Basoda (24,306 votes) blocks [cite: 6526, 6527, 6528].

#### Left and Socialist Factions
* **Socialist Party:** Contested Guna in 1951-52 (6,592 votes) and Kurwai (1,452 votes) unsuccessfully [cite: 6460, 6461, 6462]. Tiny entries in 1967 and 1972 routinely lost their deposits [cite: 6463, 6465].
* **Praja Socialist Party (PSP):** Absent from Lok Sabha, it put up losing candidates in the Bhilsa bye-elections and the 1962 Vidisha Reserved seat (589 votes) [cite: 6466, 6467, 6468, 6469].
* **Samyukta Socialist Party (SSP):** Filed a single candidate in Sironj (1967), collapsing with only 363 votes [cite: 6471, 6472].
* **Communist Party of India (CPI):** Filed two candidates in the 1951-52 Bhilsa Two-Member block, drawing 1,350 and 601 votes [cite: 6475, 6476]. Re-contested Kurwai (943 votes) and Vidisha (1,113 votes) unsuccessfully in 1962 [cite: 6477, 6478].
* **Rama Rajya Parishad:** Filed minor candidatures in 1951-52 (Bhilsa General: 1,999 votes; Basoda: 172 votes) and 1957, permanently abandoning campaigns thereafter [cite: 6508, 6509, 6510, 6512].
* **Swatantra Party:** Entered a single candidate in Sironj (1962), collapsing with 360 votes [cite: 6514, 6516].

---

### Journalism and Media
The regional history of the press commenced in **1956** with the launch of *Nai Halchal*, an Urdu/Hindi monthly printed at Sironj, which later shifted to Vidisha and turned into a fortnightly [cite: 6532, 6533].

A sequence of localized weeklies emerged subsequently from Vidisha town: *Vidisha Sandesh* (August 1959), *Chingari* (September 1959), *Vajranad* (1959), *Reeti-neeti* (1965), and *Anam* (1967) [cite: 6534, 6535]. Most of these independent local sheets had short lifespans; a more stable weekly titled *Lokadhwaj* commenced publication at Vidisha in 1970 [cite: 6538, 6539].

The reading public relies heavily on outside publications [cite: 6540]:
* **English Dailies & Periodicals:** Wide circulation of *Times of India*, *Hindustan Times*, *Patriot*, *Indian Express*, and *National Herald* [cite: 6541]. State-level English papers printed at Bhopal—*The Hitavada* and *Madhya Pradesh Chronicle*—hold deep reach [cite: 6542]. Popular weeklies include *Blitz*, *Illustrated Weekly*, *Screen*, *Femina*, *Filmfare*, and *Link* [cite: 6543].
* **Hindi Dailies & Magazines:** Dominated by *Hindustan*, *Navabharat Times*, *Veer Arjun*, *Nai Dunia* (Indore), *Navabharat* (Bhopal), *Bhaskar* (Bhopal), *Swadesh* (Indore), *Madhya Desh*, *Jagaran*, and *Avantika* (Ujjain) [cite: 6545]. Hindi weekly and monthly magazines include *Dharmayuga*, *Dinamana*, *Panchajanya*, *Sarika*, and *Sarita* [cite: 6546, 6547].
* **Linguistic Minorities:** Marathi readers subscribe to *Loksatta* and *Maharashtra Times* [cite: 6548]. Urdu-knowing communities subscribe to *Dawat* (Bhopal) and *Pratap* (Delhi) [cite: 6548].

---

### Prominent Voluntary Social Service Organisations
1.  **Sarvajanik Vachanalaya, Vidisha:** Initially established by young town volunteers in 1930, registered in 1938 [cite: 5803, 5804, 6552]. Housed inside a room of the personal dharmashala of Laxmichand Jain until 1952 [cite: 6553]. The Gwalior State Town Improvement Trust donated land at Tilak Square, and an aggregate public building fund donation of Rs. 45,000 completed the ground-floor structure in 1952, with full completion by 1959 [cite: 6554, 6555]. Maharaja Jiwajirao Scindia donated Rs. 25,000 for the building fund and an additional Rs. 25,000 as a permanent endowment reserve [cite: 6556]. The library section carries over **6,500 books** in multiple languages, serving around 70,000 readers annually, financed via the rental income of ground-floor municipal shops [cite: 6557, 6559, 6560].
2.  **Mahila Kalyan Kendra, Vidisha:** Established in 1940 to secure child and women welfare, registered in 1958 [cite: 6563]. It runs an active craft training facility and condensed higher secondary education classes for adult women, benefiting 70 women annually [cite: 6563, 6564]. Supported by the Central Social Welfare Board and the MP state education department, it runs a dedicated protective shelter home/hostel for destitute, discarded, or dejected women and helpless widows [cite: 6567, 6568].
3.  **Shri Akhil Vishva Jain Mission (Branch Sironj):** Established at Sironj in 1950 as a direct branch of the Aliganj central mission (Etah, UP) [cite: 6570]. Aims to propagate vegetarianism, non-violence (*Ahimsa*), and spiritual knowledge via the free distribution of pamphlets and books financed by private donors [cite: 6570, 6571]. It enrolls subscribers for the mission's baseline organs: *Voice of Ahimsa* (English) and *Ahimsa-Vani* (Hindi), maintaining an open reading room and distributing food, clothes, and medical aid to the poor via 100 honorary, non-salaried local members [cite: 6573, 6574, 6575, 6576, 6577].

---

## CHAPTER XIX: PLACES OF INTEREST

### Badoh
An archaeologically monumental village in Kurwai Tahsil, situated 19 km east of the **Kulhar Railway Station** via a metalled road to Pathari [cite: 6580, 6581, 6583, 6584]. Positioned 30 km south-east of Kurwai, its historical remains encircle a massive ancient lake [cite: 6585, 6588]. In medieval times, its urban boundaries encompasses the adjacent village of Pathari [cite: 6589].
* **Gadarmal Temple:** A colossal, soaring 9th-century shrine situated south-east of a local ridge, visible across miles [cite: 6590, 6591, 6595]. Features an oblong cell with an ornate entrance porch but lacks a *sabha mandapa* (resembling the *Teli-ka-mandir* of Gwalior Fort) [cite: 6593, 6594]. The shikhara represents a subsequent heterogeneous restoration executed by the Jains using structural fragments pilfered from adjacent ruined temples [cite: 6595, 6596, 6609, 6610]. Historically, a colossal stone statue of a mother goddess with a divine infant stood inside (Cunningham associated it with Trishala/Mahavira due to Jain bed-stead lion motifs; others linked it to Maya Devi or Devaki) [cite: 6597, 6601, 6602]. The original colossus was broken, but fragments were retrieved from debris and shifted to the Gwalior Museum [cite: 6599, 6602, 6606]. Side pedestals carry socket mortises for missing idol rows [cite: 6607, 6608]. The perimeter contains remnants of seven minor shrines (including a Sun Temple containing a *Navagraha* panel and *Ashta-shakti* friezes), unique bas-relief hunting scenes, and an exquisite but ruined *Torana* gateway whose columns historically bore capitals formed of intertwined women and lions [cite: 6615, 6617, 6618, 6619, 6620].
* **Jain Temple Complex:** Positioned to the west of Gadarmal, it contains **25 small rock-cut shrines/cells** arranged in a square enclosure around a central open pillared mandapa, built between the 9th and 12th centuries [cite: 6622, 6623, 6624]. A lithic inscription dated Vaishakha 14, Samvat 933 (876 A.D.) records the name of Padu-kulatilaka, the heir-apparent of Malwa [cite: 6625, 6627]. The cells shelter icons of the 24 Tirthankaras under shikharas, domes, and flat stone slabs [cite: 6628, 6629, 6630].
* **Dashavatara Temple & Sola Khambi:** Positioned north of the lake, featuring a group of 8th to 10th-century shrines and an open pillared hall dedicated to the ten avatar incarnations of Vishnu [cite: 6631, 6634, 6635]. Nearby bank lines carry 9th-century *Sati pillars* carved with seated *Hara-Gauri* icons, dancers, and musicians [cite: 6635, 6636]. The **Sola-Khambi** is a flat-roofed Gupta-era building measuring 7.62 meters square, resting on a 1.52-meter high moulded plinth supported by 16 massive square stone columns [cite: 6640, 6641, 6642].
* **Sat Madhi Temples:** Located 1.5 km north-east of Dashavatara, containing a group of six contemporary Shaivite and Vaishnavite shrines, including a Ganesha cell and three seated stone statues of Lord Buddha (as the 9th avatar of Vishnu) [cite: 6643, 6644, 6645, 6646, 6647].

---

### Basoda & Bes (Ancient Vidisha / Besnagar)
* **Basoda:** Redefined Class III municipal town and tahsil headquarters located on the Bombay-Delhi rail main line, acting as one of the preeminent grain mandis and cattle-fair centers of Malwa [cite: 6651, 6652, 6655, 6657, 4968]. Fully electrified, housing a 1971 population of 20,443 across 818 hectares [cite: 6657, 6661].
* **Bes (Besnagar / Wessanagara):** A globally renowned archaeological site situated 3 km west of the Vidisha rail station in the fork between the perennial Betwa and Bes (Halali) rivers [cite: 6663, 6664, 6665, 6666]. The ancient capital of Dasharna spanned a circuit perimeter of 8 km [cite: 6671, 6673, 691]. Buddhist literature records that **Prince Ashoka** halted here on his route to Ujjain, marrying **Devi**, the banker's daughter [cite: 6674, 6676]. Wealthy Besnagar merchants financed the magnificent stupas at Sanchi [cite: 6678]. The subsequent medieval town of Bhilsa was structurally built by tearing down and pilfering stone blocks from Besnagar's fortification lines [cite: 6680, 6781].

#### Cunningham's Explorations (1814-1877)
Alexander Cunningham unearthed portions of an ancient Ashokan Buddhist stupa rail to the east of the village, including a 2-meter long curved coping-stone, rail bars, and columns bearing 3rd-century B.C. Brahmi donor scripts [cite: 6681, 6682, 6683, 6684, 6685]. Friezes depicted a relic-box procession of four elephants, horsemen with offering trays, and a *Dharma Chakra* wheel [cite: 6686, 6687, 6692, 6693].

He picked up 90 ancient coins, including 6 punch-marked silver specimens, 50 Eran/Ujjain type cast coins, 8 Western Satrap pieces, 8 Naga coins, and a golden piece of Chandragupta II [cite: 6698, 6699, 6700]. He found a massive 2-meter high female statue, a lion capital, a Kalpa-drum capital (divine wish-fulfilling tree), and the celebrated **Kham Baba column** [cite: 6701, 6702].

#### The Heliodoros Pillar (Kham Baba Site)
Excavations in 1913-14 by the state department confirmed that the quartzilic sandstone monolith was erected around **140 B.C.** by **Heliodora (Heliodoros)**, son of Diya (Dion), a Greek envoy sent from the Indo-Bactrian King Antalikita (Antialkidas) of Taxila to the court of the local Sunga Prince Bhagabhadra [cite: 6704, 6705, 6707, 6708, 6709]. Heliodoros styled himself a *Bhagavata*, making this the **earliest known structural monument of the Vasudeva (Vaishnava) sect** [cite: 6708, 6714, 6715].

The monolith is pinkish-brown, lacks Mauryan mirror polish, and rises via a multifaceted shaft: an octagonal base with half-lotus designs carrying two opposite Brahmi inscriptions; an intermediate 16-sided middle band adorned with festoons of custard-apples, mangoes, and brinjals; a 32-sided neck; and a circular top [cite: 6716, 6717, 6719, 6720, 6721, 6722, 6723, 6724, 6725]. The bell capital bears an abacus decorated with confronted geese feeding and honeysuckle curves, originally surmounted by a figure of **Garuda** [cite: 6726, 6729, 6730, 6731].

A separate broken column shaft found in a Vidisha street confirms that another matching Garuda column was erected in the 12th year of Maharaja Bhagavata by Gotami's son [cite: 6744, 6747, 6748]. A four-armed stone Garuda statue of the Chandragupta II era (5th century A.D.) was retrieved nearby, matching the column's abacus dimensions [cite: 6752, 6757, 6758, 6759]. The land was later gifted as an *Inam* to a Shaivite priest; the column is anointed with red-lead and oil as a deity of the *Dhimar/Bhoi* fishermen [cite: 6762, 6763, 6764].

#### Discoveries of Canal, Kundas, and Seals
* **Pre-Mauryan Irrigation Canal:** Excavations below the stone rail lines exposed an ancient brick storage canal system infilled with clean alluvial flood soil, proving that the Bes river was systematically dammed to feed fields in the pre-Mauryan era [cite: 6785, 6786, 6787, 6790, 6792]. Terracotta lions, a brass signet ring reading *Golakasa* in reverse 1st-century B.C. Brahmi, and miniature bells were found [cite: 6805, 6807, 6811].
* **Sacrificial Kundas & Assembly Halls:** Digging at a small eastern mound exposed three early 4th-century A.D. brick sacrificial pits (*Kundas*) arranged in square, oblong, and *Yoni* profiles, alongside coins of Kshatrapa Ishvaradatta and Naga Kings (Bhima and Ganapati) [cite: 6812, 6814, 6817, 6818, 6819]. Brick drainage canals with stone bottoms ran from the pits [cite: 6820]. Concurrently, two large structures—one 35 meters long and another 19 meters long—were identified as an **Assembly Hall** and a **Sacrificial Dining Hall** littered with vast quantities of broken feast pottery [cite: 6823, 6830, 6831, 6832, 6834].
* **Incised Clay Seals:** The hall floors yielded **26 incised clay document seals** bearing string/tablet impressions on their backs [cite: 6836, 6837]. The Brahmi legends carry technical sacrificial Vedic terms like *hota*, *pota*, and *mamantra*, alongside a central donor name **Timitra**—the Sanskritized form of the Greek name **Demetrius** [cite: 6838, 6839].
* **Discovery of True Lime Mortar:** Below the Andhra and Sunga stone lines, brick walls inside the canal and halls utilized advanced **true lime mortar as a cementing material**, shattering previous archaeological assumptions that lime mortar was unknown in India before the Islamic era [cite: 6841, 6844, 6845, 6846]. Mauryan ring-wells and a flat stone incised with *bhikhuniya* ("by the nun") were located at the lowest levels [cite: 6847, 6848, 6851].
* **1963-1975 Stratigraphy & Rubble Wall:** Stratified excavations on the river confluence split into six distinct cultural layers, ranging from pre-NBP black-and-red wares to Sunga seals reading *Nikumbha-ragasya*, up to Gupta stamped motifs [cite: 6863, 6864, 6865, 6868, 6870]. In December 1975, a massive **10-meter wide rubble fortification wall** of the 2nd century B.C. was exposed [cite: 6876, 6880, 6881, 6882]. Built using an economical, high-strength criss-cross method (1-meter thin parallel walls packed internally with brickbats, gravel, and rubble), it sat 3.8 meters deep near a dozen charred wooden posts [cite: 6883, 6884, 6887, 6888]. Heavy 10 kg catapult stone balls were recovered near the parapets, indicating machine propulsion defenses [cite: 6889, 6891]. Artifacts included a stone Ganesa head, a terracotta plaque of Siva on Nandi, a lower half of a Mahisa-mardini icon, and an octagonal Sunga stone column with a *makara* capital [cite: 6892, 6893, 6895].

---

### Gyaraspur
A historically rich village situated in a steep gorge of low ridges, 38.4 km north-east of Vidisha along the old Sagar road [cite: 6909, 6910, 6911, 6912]. Its ruins combine Buddhist, Brahmanical, and Jain heritages [cite: 6913, 6914].
* **Athakhamba & Bajra Matha:** The Athakhamba represents the structural remnant of a grand 10th-century temple, consisting of eight highly carved square stone columns supporting an open antechamber doorway with a trefoil arch, carrying a pilgrim's record dated Samvat 1039 (982 A.D.) [cite: 6919, 6920, 6921]. The **Bajra Matha** is a rare triple-shrine temple where three cellas stand abreast [cite: 6923]. Though now occupied by Digambara Jain idols, basement and lintel carvings prove it was originally built for the **Hindu Trinity**: the central cella for Surya, the southern for Vishnu, and the northern for Siva [cite: 6924, 6925, 6926].
* **Maladevi Temple:** The largest and most spectacular monument at Gyaraspur, picturesquely cut directly out of the slope of a ridge and supported by a massive stone retaining wall [cite: 6931, 6932, 6933]. It comprises a large porch, a central pillared hall, and a sanctum encircled by a circumambulatory loop, crowned with a soaring, heavy shikhara covered in exquisite carvings [cite: 6934]. Now housing Jain icons, outer dedicatory blocks display a Hindu goddess, showing subsequent sectarian appropriation [cite: 6935].
* **Hindola Torana & The Shalbhanjika:** An ornamental free-standing entrance archway (*Torana*) which historically led to a massive, lost temple of Vishnu [cite: 6936, 6937]. The two upright pillars and cross-beam mimic a swing (*Hindola*), carved across all four faces with panels containing the ten avatar incarnations of Vishnu [cite: 6938, 6939]. Adjacent columns carried capitals carved into multi-headed lions and elephants [cite: 6940]. Gyaraspur is the source of the world-famous **Shalbhanjika** sculpture (8th century, now preserved in the Gwalior Fort Museum), a matchless masterpiece of oriental art depicting a tree-nymph (*Vrikshaka*) in a complex *tribhanga* triple-flexion posture with intense, fluid facial expressions [cite: 6953, 6957, 6958, 6959, 6960].
* **Buddhist Stupas & Gond Fortress:** Dry rubble masonry platforms atop the northern ridge represent ransacked remnants of ancient Buddhist stupas where a worn icon of Lord Buddha was located, alongside two other Buddha reliefs carved into a cliff 3 km west [cite: 6942, 6944, 6948, 6949, 6950]. A hillside compound houses remnants of a ruined 8th-century Vishnu shrine displaying *Garuda* on its lintel [cite: 6963, 6965, 6966]. The **Gyaraspur Garhi** (fortress) and the *Manasarovar* lake were built by the Gond Chief Mansingh in the 17th century, later modified by Muslim governors [cite: 6966, 6968, 6969]. An English Christian tomb honors Sergeant Major John Snow of the 72nd Regiment, who died here on 8th October, 1837 [cite: 6971]. The village runs an annual 12-day Ramlila fair [cite: 6973].

---

### Kurwai, Lateri, and Pathari
* **Kurwai:** Situated on the right bank of the Betwa, 77 km north-east of Vidisha; its railway station is Kurwai-Kethora on the main trunk line [cite: 6981, 6983, 6984, 6985]. Its prime monument is a hill fort built entirely of **local gneiss rock**, which is also used as thick building slabs for town houses [cite: 6987, 6988, 6989].
* **Lateri (Badi and Chhoti Madagaon):** Tahsil headquarters located 29 km south-west of Sironj [cite: 6993, 6994, 6995]. **Badi Madagaon** houses old rock-cut caves situated near a 45-meter seasonal waterfall and two lakes [cite: 6998, 6999, 7000]. **Chhoti Madagaon** features a 10th-century *Neelakantheshwara Temple* holding broken sculptures styled identically to the great temple of Udaipur [cite: 7001, 7002, 7003]. Lateri runs a massive 25-day commercial and religious fair every December [cite: 7005, 7008].
* **Jhookarjogi:** A Lateri tahsil village housing ancient rock-cut caves in its ridges [cite: 6975, 6977].
* **Pathari & The Rashtrakuta Pillar:** A Kurwai tahsil village built on a sandstone ridge, historically part of the ancient urban complex of Badoh before its destruction by Aurangzeb [cite: 7013, 7014, 7019, 7020, 7021, 7022]. It houses a spectacular white sandstone monolith pillar known as **Bhimagada**, standing **14.30 meters high** (with 13 meters executed from a single block) [cite: 7023, 7024, 7028]. The column bears a bell capital and a 38-line northern inscription confirming it was erected as a *Garudadwaja* in Samvat 917 (**861 A.D.**) by the chief minister of the **Rashtrakuta King Parabala** in front of a Vishnu temple [cite: 7024, 7025, 7026, 7027]. The doorway displays a relief of Garuda [cite: 7027].
* **Sapta Matrikas and Varaha:** A rock-cut panel depicting the **Sapta Matrikas (Seven Mothers)** is sculptured into the southern face of the ridge, dated to the **5th century A.D.** and bearing an inscription of King Jayatsena [cite: 7034, 7035]. To the east sits a colossal, unfinished *Varaha* animal form carved directly into a giant freestanding boulder [cite: 7035, 7036]. An old stepwell (*baori*) carries a lithic slab recording its 1676 construction under Maharaja Prithviraj Ju Deva during the reign of Aurangzeb [cite: 7037].

---

### Shamshabad & Sironj
* **Shamshabad:** Positioned 38 km north-west of Vidisha along the Sanpan river [cite: 7041, 7042, 7043, 7045]. Shams Khan built a palace and a mosque here in 1641 A.D., followed by the construction of the *Shamsgarh Fort* in the 18th century [cite: 7045, 7046]. Shams Khan treacherously murdered the Thakur of Tor to seize his lands, but was subsequently killed in battle by the Thakur's grandson Bakhtawar Singh with the military assistance of Dost Muhammad Khan of Bhopal [cite: 4248, 7047, 7048, 7049]. Shams Khan was buried inside the ruined fort [cite: 7049].
* **Sironj:** Located north-west of Vidisha, connected via Kurwai-Kethora rail line [cite: 7057, 7058, 7060]. It holds deep historical value: its oldest monument is a 2-meter high subterranean stone icon of Chandranatha (the 8th Tirthankar) located at Dharampur, bearing an inscription dated Samvat 1112 (**1055 A.D.**), locally called *Kali Telan* [cite: 7064, 7065, 7066, 7068]. Another Jain excavation spot called *Nisaiya* yielded a broken statue of Mahavira [cite: 7069, 7070]. The town houses the 11th-century *Girdhari Temple* (famed for fine carvings), the forest shrines of Jatashankara (where Tatya Tope camped in 1857), and a *Neelakantheshwara Temple* built by **Ahilyabai Holkar in 1771**, which sits near a four-pillared stone monument called **Udan Chhatri (flying cenotaph)** [cite: 7072, 7073, 7074, 7077, 7080, 7081, 7082].

#### Industrial Decline of Sironj
During the 17th century, Sironj was populated by multi-generational merchant guilds and artisans, producing vibrant **calicoes and chintzes** exported to Persia and Turkey for bed-covers and garments [cite: 7090, 7091, 7095]. The brilliant, fast character of Sironj vegetable dyes was attributed to the properties of the local river Nahen [cite: 7098].

It was equally famous for a superfine muslin fabric described by Tavernier as being so transparently fine that the skin showed completely through it, causing governors to ban its private export and reserve the entire output exclusively for the Great Mughal's seraglio and inner court nobles [cite: 7100]. Sir Thomas Roe (1615-16) noted that Sironj cloth exceeded all other kingdoms in fineness, and Ralph Fitch (1583-91) called it a massive cotton trading hub, while William Finch documented extensive betel-leaf orchards [cite: 7104, 7105, 7106].

Following Akbar's rule, Sironj was the site where Chhatrasal attacked and wiped out an imperial Mughal force [cite: 7107, 7108]. Acquired by Peshwa Baji Rao I, it was formalized under the Peshwa via the **Treaty of Sironj (1738)** signed with Nizam-ul-Mulk, who then granted it to the Holkars, who eventually passed it to Amir Khan in 1798 to form the state of Tonk [cite: 7109, 7110, 7118]. A copper coin mint operated here until 1892 [cite: 7118].

---

### Teonda, Udayagiri, and Udayapur
* **Teonda:** A historic village in Basoda Tahsil, situated near a ridge overlooking a large tank, scattered with ruined palaces and mosques built during the Malwa Sultanate using stone components from older classical structures [cite: 7122, 7123, 7124, 7126, 7127].
* **Udayagiri Caves:** An internationally celebrated site located 6 km north-west of Vidisha on an isolated sandstone ridge 110 meters high, stretching between the Bes and Betwa rivers [cite: 7131, 7132, 7133, 7135, 7136]. It features **20 rock-cut cave temples** documenting a synthesis of Buddhist, Brahmanical, and Jain heritages [cite: 7134, 7137, 7143].
    * *Cave No. 1 & 20 (Jain):* Cave 1 represents the earliest evolution of standard Indian temple architecture, planned with a small square sanctum and a simple columned front porch, housing a standing silhouette icon cut from solid rock [cite: 7144, 7145, 7148]. Cave 20 sits on the north-eastern crest, dedicated to Parshvanatha; its internal chamber is split into 5 compartments and carries an 8-line Gupta script dated **Gupta Era 106 (272 A.D.)** *[sic]* [cite: 7149, 7150, 7151, 7152, 7153].
    * *Brahmanical Caves:* Caves 2, 3, and 13 (housing a massive rock-cut reclining *Sheshshayi Vishnu*) are highly decorated [cite: 7155, 7189]. Cave 4 is titled the **Veena Cave**, featuring an ornamental doorway where a boss relief depicts a musician playing the Indian lute (*Veena*) and another playing a stringed guitar (*Sarangi*), adjacent to an *Ashta Shakti* panel [cite: 7156, 7157, 7158, 7159].
    * *Cave No. 5 (Varaha Avatara):* Houses a world-famous, monumental rock-cut relief of **Varaha**, the third boar incarnation of Vishnu [cite: 7161, 7162]. The open cliff cutting is 6.5 meters long and 4 meters high, depicting the boar-headed god stepping his left foot onto the coiled body of the *Naga* King under a 13-headed serpent canopy [cite: 7163, 7164]. With his right tusk, he lifts the slender form of *Prithvi* (Earth) out of a wavy ocean background, symbolizing order rising from chaos [cite: 7165, 7166]. Friezes depict rows of witnessing Devas and Asuras, alongside the descent of the personified river goddesses **Ganga and Yamuna** holding water jars, entering the ocean received by Varuna [cite: 7167, 7170, 7171, 7172, 7173]. This represents the pinnacle of classical Gupta sculpture [cite: 7174].
    * *Caves 6, 7, and Tawa Cave:* Cave 6 carries a prominent 2-line historical inscription of the emperor **Chandragupta II dated Gupta Era 82 (401 A.D.)**, cut over pilaters flanking reliefs of Vishnu and Mahishasuri Devi [cite: 7176, 7177, 7179, 7180]. The **Tawa Cave** is an isolated rock mass cut into a hemispherical stupa shape with a square base, crowned by a massive flat disc stone resembling a griddle (*tawa*) [cite: 7183, 7184]. It contains a 5-line inscription by Chandragupta’s minister who accompanied the king on a military campaign, dedicating the cell to Shambhu (Siva), with an internal roof carved into a large lotus [cite: 7185, 7187]. Cave 11 (*Amrita*) features a lingam which a 1036 A.D. pilgrim text confirms was originally a Vishnu shrine, displaying an intricate ceiling panel of the **Churning of the Ocean** (*Samudra Manthan*) [cite: 7191, 7192, 7194, 7196]. Base lines carry Buddhist rail-bars and a lion capital preserved in the Gwalior Museum [cite: 7204, 7206, 7207].

#### 3. Udayapur & The Udayeshwara Temple
A historic municipal village in Basoda Tahsil located 6.5 km east of Bareth Station, surrounded by an ancient fortification wall built of giant uncemented stone blocks [cite: 7210, 7211, 7212, 7217]. Old Sanskrit inscriptions confirm the town was founded by the **Paramara King Udayaditya** in the late 11th century A.D [cite: 4219, 7219].

The **Udayeshwara Temple** was constructed by his direct order between **1059 A.D.** (construction launch) and **1080 A.D.** (flagstaff erection) [cite: 7220, 7222, 7224]. The Indo-Aryan masterpiece sits in a spacious courtyard enclosed by a dwarf stone wall with rear seats, accessed via four ornate stone entry gates guarded by *Dwarpala* keepers [cite: 7224, 7225, 7226, 7229]. Out of 8 original attendant shrines, 6 stand in ruins [cite: 7230]. The inner cella houses a massive stone *lingam* completely covered in a protective **sheet of solid brass presented in 1775 by a Maratha general of Mahadji Sindhia** [cite: 7231, 7232].

Porch seats are covered in historical Sanskrit inscriptions [cite: 7234]. The exterior walls are profusely carved with detailed icons of Brahma, Vishnu, Ganesha, and Kshetrapalas, though figures of Siva and Durga dominate [cite: 7235]. The tall, vertical spire is decorated with miniature repeating ribs and medallions [cite: 7236, 2238].

Sultan Muhammad Tughluq (1336-38) mutilated many carvings and demolished sections of the attendant cells to build a **Mosque at the back of the temple**, recorded via two internal Persian inscriptions [cite: 7239, 7240]. The sculpture style is finer than Khajuraho [cite: 7243, 7244]. King Udayaditya also excavated a massive adjacent reservoir lake called *Udayasamudra* [cite: 7245].

Other Udayapur structures include:
* **Bijamandal (Ghadiyalan-ka-makan):** A ruined two-storeyed Paramara house whose name confirms it housed the official **time-keeper / clockman** attached to the main temple, opening with a Sanskrit praise to the Sun-god [cite: 7246, 7247, 7248, 7249].
* **Barakhambi & Pisanari-ka-Mandir:** An 11th-century pillared hall [cite: 7250, 7253]. The *Pisanari-ka-mandir* is a temple remnant traditionally built by a poor peasant woman exclusively out of her minor daily earnings accumulated by **grinding flour for the master stone-masons** working on the main Udayeshwara temple [cite: 7257].
* **Shahi Mosque and Mughal Mansion:** An imposing Mughal mosque whose Persian text confirms construction launched under Jahangir and completed under Shah Jahan in **1632 A.D.**, adjacent to a governor's mansion featuring delicate stone *Jali* screens [cite: 7261, 7262, 7263, 7264]. Outside the eastern gate sits the *Sherkhan ki Masjid*, built in **1488 A.D.** under Sultan Ghias Shah Khilji of Mandu [cite: 7268]. Nearby sits the *Ghod-daud-ki-baodi*, a large stepwell with wide, shallow steps designed specifically to allow **cavalry horses to run directly down to the water level** [cite: 7268].

---

### Modern Vidisha Town
The district headquarters was officially named **Bhilsa** until 1956, when it was renamed **Vidisha** due to its proximity to the ancient capital [cite: 7273, 7274, 7275]. Located on the eastern bank of the Betwa, it is encircled by heavy square stone walls constructed using materials pillared from Besnagar, pierced by three gates [cite: 7276, 7282, 7283, 7284].

Following the 8th-century abandonment of Besnagar, the population established Bhilsa, naming it after the **Bhillasvamin Sun Temple** [cite: 7288, 7292]. Lithic scripts confirm this Sun Temple was active between 939 and 968 A.D., built by a minister during the reign of the Rashtrakuta King Krishna III [cite: 7293, 7294, 7295]. Chandella texts of 1133 A.D. call it *Bhasvat* [cite: 7295]. The Tabaqat-i-Nasiri logs that the great temple was destroyed by Iltutmish around 1234 A.D., recorded as measuring a soaring 105 cubits (*Gaz*) in height [cite: 7298, 892]. Jain texts call it *Bhadalpur*, the birthplace of Sheetalanath [cite: 7306].

* **Lohangi Rock:** An isolated, flat-topped sandstone volcanic plug near the rail station, rising 6 meters [cite: 7308, 7310, 7311]. Its top houses a low mosque bearing two Persian inscriptions: one from Mahmud Khilji I of Malwa dated 1460 A.D. and another from Akbar dated 1583 A.D [cite: 7311, 7312]. It holds the tomb of the Sufi saint Lohangi-Pir (Sheikh Jalal Chishti) and a giant Buddhist pillar capital hollowed into a water basin called **Pani-ki-Kundi** [cite: 7313, 7314].
* **Gumbaz-ka-maqbara & Bijamandal Mosque:** A single-domed 1487 A.D. merchant tomb with a verandah [cite: 7316, 7317, 7318]. The **Bijamandal Mosque** sits directly on the foundations of the 11th-century *Vijay Mandir* temple (originally dedicated to the goddess Charchika or Vijaya) [cite: 7320, 7321, 7322]. Aurangzeb demolished the shrine in **1682** to build the mosque out of its components [cite: 7321]. A massive annual *Nagpanchami fair* gathers around the site [cite: 7328]. Industrial assets include processing dal/oil mills and the *MP Board and Paper Mill* [cite: 7329].

### TABLE I: Normals and Extremes of Rainfall (Based on Data up to 1961)
* **Vidisha:** Normal Rainfall: 1,159.7 mm; Average annual rainy days: 51.4; Heaviest 24-hour rainfall: 328.9 mm (September 7, 1939) [cite: 7386, 7387, 7388].
* **Basoda:** Normal Rainfall: 1,141.1 mm; Average annual rainy days: 46.9; Heaviest 24-hour rainfall: 360.2 mm (July 31, 1951) [cite: 7391, 7396].
